# Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI

NVIDIA

###### Abstract

Abstract.

We introduce Nemotron 3.5 Super and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, open and
efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer models designed
for reasoning, long-context understanding, and agentic workloads. Super
is a 120B-parameter, 12B-active multimodal model, while Lightning is a
smaller 30B-parameter, 3B-active text model; both designed for efficient
deployment. Both models combine large-scale distillation, long-context
training, and native speculative decoding, while Super further
incorporates Indic continued pretraining and agentic
software-engineering mid-training.

We develop a comprehensive post-training pipeline spanning supervised
fine-tuning and reinforcement learning (RLVR and RLHF), complemented in
Super by specialized teacher training and multi-teacher on-policy
distillation (MOPD). This transforms Super from a text-only base model
into a multimodal agent capable of reasoning over text, images, video,
and interactive tool environments. We further explore test-time scaling
through parallel agent execution and self-verification.

Across training and deployment, we jointly optimize capability and
efficiency. Indic continued pretraining improves Indian-language
benchmarks by up to 9.82 percentage points, while agentic mid-training
strengthens software-engineering capabilities and robustness across
agent harnesses. The post-trained Super model achieves 51.78% on
SWE-bench Pro, surpassing Nemotron 3 Ultra despite using approximately
4.6× fewer active parameters. Post-training MTP boosting improves
speculative-decoding acceptance length by up to 21.8% under
probabilistic draft sampling, while NVFP4 quantization achieves 99.62%
median accuracy recovery relative to BF16. Inference optimizations
enable up to 4,856 output tokens/s/GPU on GB200 while sustaining at
least 50 tokens/s/user. Under the evaluated interactive agentic
workload, NVFP4 achieves 2.43$`\times`$ the throughput of BF16, while
Nemotron 3.5 Super delivers 2.34$`\times`$ and 1.90$`\times`$ the
throughput of Qwen3.5 and Qwen3.8 Flash-Next, respectively. We release
base, BF16 post-trained, and NVFP4-quantized model checkpoints,
alongside standalone parallel speculative drafters (LiLiCorr, DFlash,
and DSpark), with accompanying model cards, pretraining and
post-training datasets, cookbooks, and training recipes.

## 1 Introduction

![Refer to caption](2610.38095v1/figures/main_figure.png)

Figure 1: Benchmark performance of Nemotron 3.5 Super (120B-A12B)
compared with six leading open models across agentic tasks, code
generation, mathematical and scientific reasoning, multimodal
understanding, long-context reasoning, and chat preference. Higher
scores indicate better performance. Full evaluation results and
methodology are provided in Section 3.8 and Table 10.

We present Nemotron 3.5 Super and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, efficient
Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer models designed for
reasoning, multimodal understanding, and long-running agentic workloads.
Nemotron 3.5 Super is a 120 billion total and 12 billion active
parameter model initialized from Nemotron 3 Super ([NVIDIA,
2026b](#bib.bib58)), while Lightning 3.5 is a 30 billion total and 3
billion active parameter model initialized from Nano 3 Base ([NVIDIA,
2025c](#bib.bib56)). As LLM applications increasingly move toward
long-horizon agents that write code, interact with tools, reason over
large contexts, and operate across multimodal environments, both model
capability and inference efficiency become increasingly important.
Nemotron 3.5 targets this regime through efficient hybrid architectures,
native speculative decoding with Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) ([Gloeckle
et al., 2024](#bib.bib27)), large-scale distillation, specialized
continued pretraining and mid-training, agent-focused post-training, and
NVFP4 quantization.

Figure
[1](#S1.F1 "Figure 1 ‣ 1 Introduction ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares Nemotron 3.5 Super against leading models across reasoning,
coding, agentic, and multimodal benchmarks.

We begin by adapting the MTP architecture for efficient recursive
speculative decoding. Nemotron 3.5 Super consolidates two non-shared MTP
heads into a single recursively shared head, while Lightning initializes
its MTP head from Nemotron 3 Super Base through architecture-aware
projection fusion and pruning. Both models undergo a dedicated 100
billion token MTP warm-up with the backbone frozen. For Super, we
additionally introduce hidden-state mixing to mitigate the
train–inference conditioning mismatch during recursive drafting. We
subsequently distill both models for 1 trillion tokens using the
next-larger Nemotron form factor as teacher: Ultra 3 Base ([NVIDIA,
2026](#bib.bib60)) for Super and Super 3 Base ([NVIDIA,
2026b](#bib.bib58)) for Lightning. Following distillation, Lightning
undergoes long-context training, while Super proceeds through additional
continued-pretraining and agentic mid-training stages.

We further improve Nemotron 3.5 Super through targeted
continued-pretraining and mid-training stages. First, we perform 100
billion tokens of Indic continued pretraining, incorporating 10 billion
tokens of synthetic and natural Indic data alongside 90 billion tokens
from the preceding training mixture. To support this stage, we use NeMo
Data Designer ([NVIDIA, 2025a](#bib.bib54); [Greco et al.,
2026](#bib.bib28)) to generate approximately 2.83 trillion synthetic
output tokens across 22 Indian languages and 34 language-script
configurations. This stage improves all five evaluated Indic benchmarks,
with gains of up to 9.82 percentage points. We then extend the model to
a 1M-token context length using 35 billion tokens of continued
pretraining, followed by 65.4 billion tokens of agentic
software-engineering mid-training at 256K context. The mid-training
mixture combines whole repositories, issue–pull-request pairs, commit
histories, build and CI logs, and agent trajectories. Controlled
evaluations using identical experimental SFT show improvements in
long-horizon software engineering and robustness across agent harnesses,
including DeepSWE pass@1 increasing from 6.4% to 10.4% and SWE-bench
Verified under the Codex harness improving from 48.4% to 61.8%. To
recover long-context capabilities affected by 256K mid-training, we
perform an additional 9 billion token recovery stage, restoring RULER-1M
([Hsieh et al., 2024](#bib.bib31)) performance from 33.5 to 81.0.

We post-train Nemotron 3.5 Super through a comprehensive pipeline
spanning Supervised Fine Tuning (SFT), Reinforcement Learning from
Verifiable Rewards (RLVR), Reinforcement Learning from Human Feedback
(RLHF), specialized teacher training, and Multi-teacher On-Policy
Distillation (MOPD). Our two-stage SFT pipeline transforms the text-only
base model into a vision-language model while jointly developing
reasoning, chat, safety, long-context, and agentic capabilities. RL
training spans diverse environments, including Olympiad-level
competitive programming and finance-agent tasks requiring SEC filing
search, document parsing, evidence retrieval, and tool use. We further
train specialized teachers for video reasoning and tracking, vision tool
use, and alignment, before consolidating their capabilities through
MOPD. Nemotron 3.5 Lightning follows a lighter post-training pipeline
consisting of single-stage text-only SFT and RLVR. The resulting Super
model achieves 51.78% on SWE-bench Pro, exceeding Nemotron 3 Ultra
despite using approximately 4.6$`\times`$ fewer active parameters.
Beyond model training, we explore test-time scaling through parallel
agent execution and self-verification, improving Terminal-Bench 2.1 from
62.7% to 71.9% and DeepSWE from 24.6% to 34.5%.

In parallel, we optimize Nemotron 3.5 for efficient inference and
deployment. Post-training MTP boosting improves average
speculative-decoding acceptance length from 4.287 to 4.718 under greedy
decoding and from 3.787 to 4.612 under probabilistic draft sampling on
SPEED-Bench ([Abramovich et al., 2026a](#bib.bib1)). We develop NVFP4
post-training quantization and mixed-precision recipes across weights,
caches, and the MTP drafter, complemented by quantization-aware
distillation, achieving 99.62% median accuracy recovery relative to BF16
across 36 benchmarks for the released quantized Super checkpoint. We
additionally optimize hybrid-state caching and GPU kernels for efficient
long-context and agentic serving, and develop standalone parallel
speculative drafters for low-concurrency, memory-bound inference. On a
single GB200 GPU, Nemotron 3.5 Super achieves up to 4,856 output
tokens/s/GPU on a 20-turn agentic proxy workload while sustaining at
least 50 tokens/s/user. Under the evaluated interactive serving
conditions, NVFP4 delivers 2.43$`\times`$ the throughput of BF16, while
Super achieves 2.34$`\times`$ and 1.90$`\times`$ the throughput of
Qwen3.5 122B-A10B and Qwen3.8 Flash-Next, respectively. We release base,
BF16 post-trained, and NVFP4-quantized model checkpoints, alongside
standalone parallel speculative drafters (LiLiCorr for Super, and DFlash
and DSpark for Lightning), with accompanying model cards, pretraining
and post-training datasets, cookbooks, and training recipes.

We organize the remainder of the report into four sections: Pretraining
(§[2](#S2 "2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
Post-training
(§[3](#S3 "3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
Quantization
(§[4](#S4 "4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
and Inference
(§[5](#S5 "5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).

## 2 Pretraining

In this section, we describe the pretraining and mid-training recipe
used to develop Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning 3.5. We first adapt and
warm up the Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) head for recursive speculative
decoding, then distill both models for 1 trillion tokens using the
next-larger Nemotron 3 model as teacher. Following distillation, both
models undergo long-context training. For Super, we additionally apply
Indic continued pretraining, perform agentic software-engineering
mid-training at 256K context, and finish with a short 1M-context
recovery stage. We also report the resulting base-model capabilities and
the effects of these stages on multilingual, long-context, and agentic
performance.

### 2.1 Multi-Token Prediction (MTP)

Nemotron 3.5 Super and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning feature native
speculative-decoding support via a Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) head,
adopting the recursively shared MTP formulation detailed in [NVIDIA
(2026b)](#bib.bib58). The MTP head functions as an integrated internal
drafter that predicts multiple future tokens from the backbone’s hidden
representations. This enables multi-token candidate verification in a
single forward pass over the backbone using rejection sampling
([Leviathan et al., 2023](#bib.bib44)) or exact token matching.

##### MTP Initialization.

To establish the MTP drafting head, we employ model-specific
initialization strategies tailored to the architectural origins of the
Super and Lightning variants:

- •
  Nemotron 3.5 Super: We initialize the MTP weights directly from the
  Nemotron 3 Super Base checkpoint (prior to long-context extension),
  which originally contained two distinct MTP heads. To consolidate
  multi-token drafting into a single, recursively shared head while
  preserving the underlying representation space, we initialize the MTP
  parameters exclusively from the first MTP head and discard the second.
- •
  Nemotron 3.5 Lightning: We initialize Lightning’s MTP head from that
  of Nemotron 3 Super Base. We first fuse the latent MoE projections
  into the experts’ up- and down-projection matrices, then prune the
  hidden size from 4096 to 2688, MoE intermediate size from 2688 to
  1856, and number of routed experts from 512 to 128 to match Lightning.

##### MTP Warmup Training.

Following initialization, we conduct a dedicated MTP warmup phase for
$`100\,\text{B}`$ tokens, freezing the backbone parameters and
optimizing only the MTP head. For Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, we applied the
standard 2-step recursive MTP training. However, this naive training
induces a pronounced train–inference conditioning mismatch. During
standard training, recursive step $`k`$ conditions on the full shifted
sequence of ground-truth hidden states generated at step $`k-1`$. In
contrast, during autoregressive drafting at inference time, the MTP head
conditions on an increasingly heterogeneous mixture of backbone hidden
states and synthetic states generated by preceding MTP steps. This
discrepancy compounds with increasing draft depth, degrading token
acceptance rates at later positions ([NVIDIA, 2026c](#bib.bib59)).

To bridge this distribution gap in Nemotron 3.5 Super, we adopt a
hidden-state-mixing strategy similar to [NVIDIA (2026c)](#bib.bib59). At
training step $`k`$, input hidden states are sampled from the sequence
of states produced by earlier MTP steps $`1,\ldots,k-1`$, rather than
relying exclusively on step $`k-1`$. Exposing the MTP head to
heterogeneous hidden-state mixtures during warmup enhances robustness
across deeper draft steps, achieving significant acceptance rate gains
without the computationally intensive multi-step simulation required by
test-time training (TTT) ([Li et al., 2025](#bib.bib46)).

### 2.2 Distillation

We initialized Super 3.5 Base from Super 3 Base ([NVIDIA,
2026b](#bib.bib58)) and Lightning 3.5 Base from Nano 3 Base ([NVIDIA,
2025c](#bib.bib56)), then distilled each model for 1 trillion tokens.

For Lightning 3.5 Base, we used top-128 logits from Super 3 Base on 1
trillion tokens of the Ultra 3 Phase 2 data ([NVIDIA,
2026c](#bib.bib59)). We used standard forward KL divergence loss along
with an extra sink token to capture the residual probability mass
outside top-128. To provide training signal for rare tokens, we combined
the KL loss with cross-entropy loss, assigning them weights of 0.9 and
0.1, respectively. To limit early drift from the initialization, we
began distillation with the minimum Learning Rate (LR) of Nano 3 Base
($`10^{-5}`$) and ramped it up slowly to $`10^{-4}`$, 10% of the Nano 3
Base’s peak LR, over 200 billion tokens. We then decayed the LR linearly
back to $`10^{-5}`$ over the remaining 800 billion tokens. All other
hyperparameters and settings match Nano 3 Base, except for the added MTP
layers. We used an MTP loss scaling factor of 0.1.

For Super 3.5 Base, we used the same distillation recipe as Lightning
3.5 Base but with Ultra 3 Base as the teacher and Skew Reverse KL
divergence ([Ko et al., 2024](#bib.bib40)) as the distillation loss
function with $`\alpha=0.1`$. Additionally, we used a smaller MoE
sequence load balancing loss coefficient of $`10^{-5}`$, as compared to
$`10^{-4}`$ for Lightning 3.5. We chose learning rates the same way as
Lightning 3.5 Base, but with Super 3 Base LRs as the anchor, using
$`4.5\times 10^{-6}`$ as the minimum and $`4.5\times 10^{-5}`$ as the
maximum.

### 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining

We augment Nemotron 3.5 Super with an Indic continued-pretraining stage
that combines synthetic text generated from English documents with
natural Indic corpora to strengthen Indian-language capabilities. Our
twelve-language synthetic-data pipeline uses Gemma-4-31B-it for
translation. We describe the data and generation pipeline, then present
Super’s training mixture and results, followed by separate Lightning 3.5
mixture ablations.

##### Indic data: motivation, coverage, and scale.

Natural Indic text is scarce in many languages, and its coverage varies
across languages, scripts, and subjects. We address these gaps by
translating curated English documents into Indic languages. Nemotron-CC
provides most of the English source documents. The sources cover general
web content, education, mathematics, STEM, and question-answering.
Keeping the source documents lets us check whether the generated text
preserves their meaning and structure.

We generated data across 22 Indian languages: native-script and
romanized text for twelve priority languages, and native-script text for
ten additional lower-resource languages. The
[Nemotron-Pretraining-Indic-v1](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-Pretraining-Indic-v1)
corpus contains a total of approximately 2.83 trillion tokens of
generated Indic text across 34 language-script configurations, counted
using a Gemma-4 tokenizer (Table
[1](#S2.T1 "Table 1 ‣ Indic data: motivation, coverage, and scale. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
The twelve priority languages contribute approximately 2.50 trillion
tokens, including 224.6 billion romanized tokens; the additional ten
languages contribute approximately 327 billion native-script tokens.
This scale and coverage provide a large, reusable pool of training text
across languages and subjects, including languages where natural text is
limited. Model builders can draw on this pool to study language
mixtures, script choices, and training budgets for their own models.

We created substantially more data than was used in Super’s Indic CPT
stage and will release the broader corpus publicly. Other teams can use
the data directly, inspect how it was produced, and extend generation to
their own needs.

|           |         |         |           |        |            |        |        |

Table 1: Reported input and output token counts by language and script,
in billions (B), using a Gemma-4 tokenizer. Input counts may overlap
across configurations; they are not unique source volume or total
pipeline processing. Subtotals sum displayed rounded values. The
output-only corpus total remains 2,825.6B (approximately 2.83T), not the
number of tokens used to train Super. The additional ten languages have
native-script data only.

##### Synthetic-data pipeline and efficiency.

We implement the four-stage pipeline for the twelve priority languages
(Figure
[2](#S2.F2 "Figure 2 ‣ Synthetic-data pipeline and efficiency. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
using [NeMo Data Designer](https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/)
([NVIDIA, 2025a](#bib.bib54); [Greco et al., 2026](#bib.bib28)).
Gemma-4-31B-it, selected based on translation-model comparisons,
performs translation, quality assessment, and rewriting. We select
source documents with fewer than 4,096 Gemma-4 tokens and translate each
document in full, without splitting, using prompts designed to preserve
facts, mathematical expressions, code, and formatting. The model then
checks each translation against its source and assigns a quality score
from 0 to 100. We keep translations scoring at least 75. Lower-scoring
translations are rewritten using the source text and identified errors,
then checked again and kept only if they pass.

Retained translations keep their source and quality metadata for
traceability. Quality scores guide filtering, although using the same
model for translation and judging may favor its own translations.

Figure 2: Four-stage Indic synthetic-data pipeline implemented using
NeMo Data Designer for the twelve priority languages. Selected English
source documents, primarily from Nemotron-CC, contain fewer than 4,096
Gemma-4 tokens and are translated in full, without splitting.
Translations are retained after passing the initial or post-rewrite
quality check. Romanization starts from native-script text and uses the
same model with script-specific prompts and quality checks. Sampled
human review helps improve prompts and checks; it does not cover every
document.

For the ten additional lower-resource languages, we use
[Bodhan-MT](https://huggingface.co/bodhan-ai/indic-translate) for
translation and Gemma-4-31B-it for quality assessment, with
language-specific acceptance thresholds. We use overlapping windows only
for longer Santali documents and convert Manipuri translations from
Bengali script to Meitei Mayek. The companion Scaling Synthetic
Pre-training Data and Evaluation for Indic LLMs report provides details.

For the twelve-language pipeline, we improved scheduling so translation
and quality checks can run concurrently. Speculative decoding, FP8
weights, and FlashAttention reduced median translation cost from 0.835
to 0.335 GPU-hours per million English input tokens, a reduction of
about 60%. This is equivalent to processing 2.49 times as many tokens
per GPU-hour. The comparison uses the same twelve-language benchmark,
with approximately 46.8M input tokens per language. The gain applies to
translation; quality checks and rewriting add further cost.

##### Data selected for Super continued pretraining.

Super’s Indic continued-pretraining run has a nominal 100B-token budget,
with 10% (10B tokens) allocated to selected synthetic text from our
corpus and natural Indic text. The remaining 90% (90B tokens) retains
the preceding training mixture’s relative source proportions. These are
configured sampling allocations, not measured per-source token totals or
counts of unique tokens in the corpus.

We chose the 10% Indic share as a practical allocation within this
budget, informed by the separate 200B-token Lightning mixture ablations
(Table
[3](#S2.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Lightning 3.5 mixture-ratio ablations. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
The 10% mixture already delivered substantial gains; higher shares
improved results further, but with smaller incremental gains.

##### Super results and capability retention.

We evaluate Indian-language knowledge, reasoning, and mathematics using
MILU and human-verified Indic translations of MMLU-Pro, ARC-Challenge,
ARC-Easy, and GSM8K.

Table
[2](#S2.T2 "Table 2 ‣ Super results and capability retention. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the distilled checkpoint immediately before Indic CPT with the
checkpoint after this stage. Scores improve across all five Indic
benchmarks, with gains ranging from 4.98 to 9.82 percentage points.
These results describe the combined continued-pretraining stage; they do
not separate the contributions of synthetic and natural Indic text.

|                              |           |                     |                  |                  |                         |
| ---------------------------- | --------- | ------------------- | ---------------- | ---------------- | ----------------------- |
| Checkpoint                   | MILU (10) | Indic MMLU-Pro (12) | Indic ARC-C (12) | Indic ARC-E (12) | Indic GSM8K strict (11) |
| Before Indic CPT (distilled) | 72.48     | 51.83               | 84.17            | 90.73            | 72.67                   |

| Change (percentage points)   | +5.42     | +4.98               | +8.66            | +6.04            | +9.82                   |

Table 2: Super performance on Indic benchmarks before and after Indic
CPT. Scores are percentages; parentheses show the number of languages.
Changes are calculated from unrounded scores.

MILU covers ten supported languages; GSM8K excludes Odia because its
evaluation exceeded the serving context limit. Translation also improves
on twelve-language IGB/FLORES: English-to-Indic chrF++ rises from 35.43
to 47.47, and Indic-to-English from 58.75 to 64.30. The later
midtraining checkpoint largely retains the Indic benchmark gains.

We also evaluated the Indic-CPT model across a broad set of English
benchmarks and observed no regressions in the tested categories.

To complement this stage-level evaluation, Figure
[3](#S2.F3 "Figure 3 ‣ Super results and capability retention. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the evaluated Nemotron 3 and 3.5 Super models on a broader set
of Indic tasks and language-quality measures. Nemotron 3.5 Super scores
higher on five of the six task metrics shown and on all four IndiVibe
dimensions. These results show the overall performance of the released
Nemotron 3.5 Super model, with Indic CPT as part of its broader training
pipeline.

Super (blue-gray) and Nemotron 3.5 Super (green). Left: six task
benchmarks. Right: preliminary IndiVibe conditional mean preference

##### Lightning 3.5 mixture-ratio ablations.

To study how the Indic share affects performance, we ran separate
Lightning 3.5 research experiments with 10%, 20%, and 30% Indic data
under a fixed 200B-token training budget. These experiments are separate
from the released Lightning 3.5 model, which did not include this Indic
CPT stage. The four-benchmark mean increased with the Indic share, with
the 30% mixture scoring highest among the tested settings (Table
[3](#S2.T3 "Table 3 ‣ Lightning 3.5 mixture-ratio ablations. ‣ 2.3 Indic Continued Pretraining ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
These experiments inform mixture design but do not establish an optimal
share for Super. Detailed language-level results, mixture
configurations, and English-retention analysis will appear in the
companion Scaling Synthetic Pre-training Data and Evaluation for Indic
LLMs report.

|                  |             |                     |
| ---------------- | ----------- | ------------------- |
| Checkpoint       | Indic share | Four-benchmark mean |
| Base reference   | -           | 45.5                |
| Mixture ablation | 10%         | 63.1                |
| Mixture ablation | 20%         | 64.6                |
| Mixture ablation | 30%         | 65.6                |

Table 3: Lightning 3.5 research ablations. Each ablation uses a
200B-token budget. The reported mean covers MILU, MMLU-Pro,
ARC-Challenge, and GSM8K. The base checkpoint is shown for reference;
the fixed-budget comparison covers the three ablation runs.

Alongside the Nemotron 3.5 technical report, we will release the broader
synthetic corpus, open-source the generation pipelines, and publish a
companion technical report. That report will provide detailed
translation-model comparisons, data-source ablations on Nemotron 3 Nano,
and Lightning 3.5 mixture experiments. In collaboration with
[AI4Bharat](https://ai4bharat.iitm.ac.in/), we will also release
human-verified Indic LLM evaluation suite. Together, these resources
will help model builders train, evaluate, and adapt models for
Indian-language applications.

### 2.4 Long Context

Similar to Nemotron 3 series, we added a long-context phase (LC-Phase)
at the end of pretraining. In the LC-Phase, we performed continued
pretraining (CPT) at 1M-token context length to equip the base model
with long-context ability. For Lightning 3.5, we used a constant
learning rate of $`1*10^{-5}`$. We used 8-way context parallelism, 8-way
tensor parallelism, 8-way expert parallelism, and 2-way pipeline
parallelism to train on GB200 GPUs. For Super 3.5, we used a constant
learning rate of $`4.5*10^{-6}`$. We used 16-way context parallelism,
8-way tensor parallelism, and 64-way expert parallelism to train on
GB300 GPUs.

We use the same data blend as Nemotron 3 Ultra ([NVIDIA,
2026c](#bib.bib59)). The LC-Phase was trained for 21B tokens for
Lightning and 35B tokens for Super. Note that for Super 3.5 after
mid-training which was performed at a context length of 256K, we
observed that the RULER-1M score dropped from 79.9 to 33.5. So we added
a short long-context recovery phase after mid-training, for which we
trained 9B tokens with the same recipe and successfully recovered the
RULER score to 81.0.

### 2.5 Mid-training

After the long-context phase, we add a mid-training stage for agentic
software engineering. Pretraining runs at short context and sees almost
no agent interactions, and the natural data around software engineering,
such as whole repositories, pull requests with their issues, commit
histories, and build logs, is often too long for it. SFT in turn
consists mostly of synthetic data, and very long SFT stages can reduce
how much the model later gains from RL. Mid-training sits between the
two. It trains the base model at long context on a diverse mixture of
natural software-engineering data with a small share of agent traces,
and leaves behavior shaping to post-training. The full recipe, including
how each data source is built and filtered and the ablations behind each
choice, is described in ([Singhal et al., 2026](#bib.bib76)).

Table
[4](#S2.T4 "Table 4 ‣ 2.5 Mid-training ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
shows the data blend we used for Super-3.5 mid-training (Lightning 3.5
did not have a mid-training stage). Most of it is natural data kept in
its original format, without chat templates. The agent traces are the
only SFT-style data, so we keep them to a small share of the blend and
mask tool responses from the loss, training only on the model’s own
turns. Every source is decontaminated against SWE-bench and
Terminal-Bench and has secrets removed.

|                    |                                                                       |       |
| ------------------ | --------------------------------------------------------------------- | ----- |
| Data source        | Description                                                           | Share |
| Issue–PR           | GitHub issues with repository context and the merged patch            | 41%   |
| Repositories       | Whole repositories, with files that import each other placed together | 20%   |
| Agentic traces     | Trajectories from many agent scaffolds, tool responses loss-masked    | 10%   |
| Commit diffs       | Multi-file commits and per-file edit histories                        | 3%    |
| Build and CI logs  | Raw build and continuous-integration logs                             | 1%    |
| Pretraining replay | Phase-2 pretraining blend                                             | 20%   |
| Long-context data  | Long-context phase data                                               | 5%    |

Table 4: Mid-training blend for Nemotron 3.5 Super, as shares of
training tokens.

We mid-trained the long-context checkpoint for 65.4B tokens at a
sequence length of 256K. We reset the learning rate, warmed it up
linearly to a maximum of $`1\times 10^{-5}`$, about twice the final
pretraining learning rate, and then held it constant for the rest of the
run. Training at 256K lowered the RULER-1M score, so we followed
mid-training with a short long-context recovery stage of about 9B
tokens, described in the Long Context section above.

To measure the effect of mid-training, we ran identical SFT on the base
checkpoint and on the mid-trained checkpoint and compared the two. This
SFT is an experimental agentic blend of about 33B tokens (2K steps), not
the one used in Section
[3](#S3 "3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
but we have found it to be a reliable signal of how a checkpoint will do
after full post-training. Table
[5](#S2.T5 "Table 5 ‣ 2.5 Mid-training ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
shows the results. The clearest gain on the standard benchmarks is on
DeepSWE, our hardest long-horizon benchmark, where pass@1 rises from 6.4
to 10.4, and BrowseComp improves slightly. SWE-bench Verified,
Multilingual, and Pro under OpenHands stay within run-to-run variation,
which is about one point. The larger effect is on robustness across
agent harnesses. Under OpenHands, the harness used for our standard
evaluations, the base model already scores well and mid-training changes
little. Under five other harnesses, the mid-trained model is better on
every one, by 8.6 points on average on SWE-bench Verified and 6.8 points
on Terminal-Bench 2.1, with the largest gain on Codex, where the base
model is weakest.

| _SWE-bench Verified, by harness_ |      |             |
| OpenHands                        | 71.3 | 71.9        |

### 2.6 Base Model Evaluations

We evaluate the base models across a broad suite of benchmarks covering
general knowledge, mathematical reasoning, code generation, commonsense
reasoning, reading comprehension, and multilingual capabilities.
Detailed evaluation settings and results are provided below.

Table 6: Base model evaluation results for Nemotron 3.5 Super 120B-A12B
Base and Lightning 3.5 30B-A3B Base. For sampled code and mathematical
reasoning benchmarks, pass@1 is estimated from 32 generations per prompt
where indicated. A dash indicates that a corresponding result is not
available.

All evaluation results reported for Nemotron 3.5 Super 120B-A12B Base
and Lightning 3.5 30B-A3B Base were collected via NeMo Evaluator SDK¹¹ 1
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Evaluator](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Evaluator)
and NVIDIA’s open-source container of LM Evaluation Harness²² 2
[https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness](https://github.com/EleutherAI/lm-evaluation-harness),
unless otherwise stated. For reproducibility, additional details on
evaluation configurations are available in the NeMo Evaluator SDK
examples³³ 3
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Evaluator/tree/main/examples](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Evaluator/tree/main/examples).
The open-source LM Evaluation Harness container packaged through
NVIDIA’s NeMo Evaluator SDK is available on NGC⁴⁴ 4
[https://catalog.ngc.nvidia.com/orgs/nvidia/teams/eval-factory/containers/lm-evaluation-harness](https://catalog.ngc.nvidia.com/orgs/nvidia/teams/eval-factory/containers/lm-evaluation-harness).

The evaluations use the following settings:

1.  1.  For general knowledge, we evaluate MMLU ([Hendrycks et al.,
        2021](#bib.bib30)), MMLU-Pro, AGIEval-En, SuperGPQA, and GPQA
        Diamond. We report exact-match metrics using benchmark-specific
        extraction procedures where applicable. GPQA Diamond is evaluated
        using 5-shot chain-of-thought prompting.
2.  2.  For mathematical reasoning, we evaluate GSM8K ([Cobbe et al.,
        2021](#bib.bib17)) using 8-shot chain-of-thought exact match and
        Minerva MATH using exact match. We additionally evaluate MATH-500
        using 4-shot sampled generation with 32 generations per prompt and
        report pass@1.
3.  3.  For code tasks, we evaluate HumanEval ([Chen et al.,
        2021](#bib.bib11)) and MBPP ([Austin et al., 2021](#bib.bib4)) using
        EvalPlus-sanitized variants ([Liu et al., 2023](#bib.bib48)). We
        report sampled pass@1 estimated from 32 generations per prompt.
        MBPP-Sanitized uses 3-shot prompting, while HumanEval uses zero-shot
        prompting. We additionally report results on LiveCodeBench CodeGen
        and the complete and instruct variants of BigCodeBench.
4.  4.  For commonsense reasoning, we evaluate CommonsenseQA, ARC-Challenge
        ([Clark et al., 2018](#bib.bib16)), HellaSwag ([Zellers et al.,
        2019](#bib.bib98)), OpenBookQA ([Mihaylov et al.,
        2018](#bib.bib53)), PIQA ([Bisk et al., 2019](#bib.bib6)),
        TruthfulQA MC2, WinoGrande ([Sakaguchi et al., 2019](#bib.bib71)),
        and SocialIQA using the accuracy or normalized-accuracy metrics
        shown in Table
        [6](#S2.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 2.6 Base Model Evaluations ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
5.  5.  For reading comprehension, we evaluate RACE using zero-shot
        accuracy.
6.  6.  For multilingual capability, we evaluate Global MMLU across German,
        Spanish, French, Italian, Japanese, Korean, Portuguese, and Chinese,
        and report the average accuracy across these eight languages. We
        evaluate MGSM ([Shi et al., 2022](#bib.bib74)) using 8-shot native
        chain-of-thought prompting in Spanish, German, French, Chinese,
        Japanese, and Russian, and report the average exact-match score
        across the six languages.

Evaluation results for Nemotron 3.5 Super 120B-A12B Base and Lightning
3.5 30B-A3B Base are summarized in Table
[6](#S2.T6 "Table 6 ‣ 2.6 Base Model Evaluations ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

## 3 Post-Training

Our post-training pipeline for Super is similar to that used in Nemotron
3 Ultra, consisting of SFT, RLVR, expert teacher model training, and
finally MOPD ([NVIDIA, 2026c](#bib.bib59)) as shown in
[4](#S3.F4 "Figure 4 ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
We elaborate on each stage of the pipeline in the following sections.
Lightning goes through a lighter pipeline, with text-only SFT, and RLVR.

![Refer to caption](2610.38095v1/figures/Super-V3.5-VL-Flow-Diagram.png)

Figure 4: Overview of the Super-V3.5-VL post-training pipeline. Teacher
models are developed from a shared trunk through supervised fine-tuning
(SFT) and reinforcement learning (RL) stages, specializing in different
capabilities. These teacher models are subsequently merged into a
unified student model through MoPD (Multi-Teacher On-Policy
Distillation).

### 3.1 Supervised Fine Tuning

We train Nemotron 3.5 Super to be a VLM and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning to be
a text only model. As the base model for Nemotron Super 3.5 is
pre-trained on text-only data, we introduce large quantities of vision
data in the SFT stage, along with agentic and reasoning text data. After
warming up the vision projector ([NVIDIA, 2026a](#bib.bib57)), we use a
two-stage SFT process because learning vision and text capabilities
simultaneously is challenging. In the first stage, we train on a general
mix of vision, reasoning, chat, long-context, safety, and agentic data.
We then do a small second stage of SFT, where we change the blend
composition to boost some capabilities the first stage did not learn
adequately. Nemotron 3.5 Lightning is trained with single SFT stage with
the text only SFT data.

Vision Data. We leveraged vision data from the Nemotron 3 Nano Omni
training corpus ([NVIDIA, 2026a](#bib.bib57)), which covers general
visual perception and VQA across charts, tables, OCR, multi-page
documents, visual grounding, GUI understanding, and video understanding.
It also covers visual reasoning over mathematics, science, and spatial
and temporal relationships, with multilingual coverage concentrated in
document and OCR tasks. We augmented this corpus with newly curated data
using open-source models such as Qwen3.5 ([Qwen Team, 2026](#bib.bib66))
to strengthen chart understanding, grounded perception, multi-hop
reasoning, GUI/web navigation, and multimodal coding. The data
generation pipeline covers diverse visual structures, domains, question
types, and reasoning tasks. We have observed that organizing the
reasoning traces into an explicit sequence of concise steps, with each
step grounded in relevant visual evidence, helps teach the model
systematic planning and encourages iterative verification against the
visual input. Additionally, we apply automated quality checks to filter
ambiguous or visually unsupported questions, incomplete reasoning
traces, hallucinated labels or values, incorrect visual or numerical
interpretations, excessive repetition, and unproductive self-correction.
Figure
[5](#S3.F5 "Figure 5 ‣ 3.1 Supervised Fine Tuning ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
summarizes the pipeline. We provide a chart-understanding implementation
of this approach as an open-source NeMo Data Designer generation
pipeline recipe⁵⁵ 5
[https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/recipes/chart-qa/chart-question-answering](https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/recipes/chart-qa/chart-question-answering)
and a companion development note⁶⁶ 6
[https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/dev-notes/chart-understanding](https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/dev-notes/chart-understanding).

Figure 5: Synthetic vision-data generation through diverse input
selection, question generation, grounded reasoning, and quality
filtering.

Agentic Vision Data. Visual tool use is a fundamental ability for
agents. To teach the model to gain this agentic ability, we generate a
comprehensive dataset spanning different areas. We use Kimi K2.6 ([Kimi
Team, 2025](#bib.bib38)) and K3 ([Kimi Team, 2026](#bib.bib39)) to
generate agentic datasets with a focus on image tool operations such as
crop, rotate, zoom, flip, etc for high-resolution images. For video
understanding, we use Kimi K2.6 ([Kimi Team, 2025](#bib.bib38)) to
generate executable SpatialClaw ([Cho et al., 2026](#bib.bib14))
demonstrations from existing video question–answer pairs, teaching the
student to collect visual evidence, use tools, and interpret
observations before answering. The source pools cover attribute
perception, temporal understanding, object reasoning, OCR, counting, and
video synopsis. Each assistant action contains Purpose, Reasoning, Next
Goal, and executable Python Code. Actions run in a persistent kernel
that returns execution feedback and visual observations. The agent can
inspect frames, query selected frames through a separate visual
question-answering call, perform SAM3 segmentation, and reconstruct
scene geometry before terminating with ReturnAnswer. We retain normally
completed trajectories whose answers match the references and whose
required tools execute successfully. Structured answers use canonical
matching; other free-form answers require same-teacher, text-only
equivalence judging with reported confidence $`\geq 0.90`$. Subsequent
filtering rejects malformed actions, execution failures, exceeded
display budgets, and trajectories lacking a successful grounded action.
We package the recorded conversations and images for SFT, removing
redundant formatting and messages. The final corpus contains 14,169
trajectories and 81,366 assistant actions. We show the performance with
SpatialClaw harness in Table
[7](#S3.T7 "Table 7 ‣ 3.1 Supervised Fine Tuning ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

|                                |          |      |            |
| ------------------------------ | -------- | ---- | ---------- |

| ERQA                           | 49.2     | 57.5 | +8.3       |
| Omni3D                         | 48.6     | 53.4 | +4.8       |

| MMSI                           | 39.3     | 43.1 | +3.8       |

| BLINK                          | 69.1     | 71.3 | +2.2       |

Baseline.

Table 7: Effect of agentic vision training on spatial reasoning and
video understanding using the SpatialClaw harness.

##### Reasoning data.

For reasoning supervised fine-tuning, we used the same underlying
reasoning problem sets as Nemotron Ultra [NVIDIA (2026)](#bib.bib60).
These problem sets span mathematics, STEM, coding, and mathematical
proof. For the mathematics and STEM problem sets, we replaced the
previous teacher with Kimi K3 ([Kimi Team, 2026](#bib.bib39)) and
regenerated both chain-of-thought (CoT) and tool-integrated reasoning
(TIR) response trajectories. Mathematical-proof trajectories were
generated using DeepSeek V4 Pro ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib18)), while
competitive-programming trajectories were generated using GLM-5.2
([GLM-5-Team et al., 2026](#bib.bib26)).

As part of data filtering, we removed reasoning responses shorter than
5,000 tokens to focus the training mixture on examples containing
sufficiently developed, multi-step reasoning. We also observed a
difference in learning dynamics between data generated by the two
primary reasoning teachers. For Nemotron Ultra, DeepSeek V4 Pro served
as the primary reasoning teacher, and downstream reasoning performance
largely plateaued after a single pass over the resulting training
trajectories. After switching the primary reasoning teacher to Kimi K3
for Nemotron 3.5 Super, performance continued to improve over multiple
passes through the regenerated trajectories. We hypothesize that the
more concise and information-dense reasoning produced by Kimi K3
requires additional exposure for a smaller student model to internalize.

Chat and Safety Data. We largely follow the same recipe as Nemotron 3
Ultra, however we refresh our chat dataset with more recent Qwen, Kimi,
and Gemma model responses. For safety data, we largely use the same
blend as Nemotron 3 Ultra, plus some additional data focused on
dual-risk CBRNE content and demographic biases.

Long-Context Data. We used the 512K long-context data from Nemotron 3
Ultra ([NVIDIA, 2026](#bib.bib60)). In addition, we synthetically
generated graph-structured data to teach the model to trace nodes and
edges through reasoning, rather than relying solely on copying and
scanning. We also found that agent trajectories from long-horizon search
and coding tasks can improve long-context generalization ([Su et al.,
2026](#bib.bib77)). Finally, for Nemotron 3.5 Super, we synthetically
generate long-context translation data from both the PG19 ([Rae et al.,
2019](#bib.bib67)) and FinePDFs-Edu ([Kydlíček et al.,
2025](#bib.bib43)) datasets where each instance is one long document
translated in chunks from the source language to a target language using
Deepseek V4 Flash ([DeepSeek-AI, 2026](#bib.bib18)). For detailed source
and target languages as well as token counts, please see Tables
[29](#A1.T29 "Table 29 ‣ Appendix A Long-Translation ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")-[30](#A1.T30 "Table 30 ‣ Appendix A Long-Translation ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
To evaluate long-translation performance, we use the SEGALE evaluation
pipeline ([Wang et al., 2025a](#bib.bib82)). We found that
long-translation performance was improved by also including synthetic
transduction tasks ([Willette et al., 2026](#bib.bib86)) that reinforce
position tracking, retrieval, mutation, and output. Long translation
results can be seen in Figure
[12](#A1.F12 "Figure 12 ‣ Appendix A Long-Translation ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
We also include long-context vision data generated with NeMo Data
Designer ([NVIDIA, 2025a](#bib.bib54)) for multi-page and whole-document
understanding over documents spanning up to hundreds of pages. These
examples require locating and combining evidence from text, tables,
charts, and diagrams across pages ⁷⁷ 7
[https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/dev-notes/vlm-long-document-understanding](https://docs.nvidia.com/nemo/datadesigner/dev-notes/vlm-long-document-understanding).

Training Config. For the SFT stage, we jointly fine-tune the language
and vision components. Training uses packed sequences of up to 524,288
tokens with a global batch size of 32. The learning rate warms up over
100 packed-sample-equivalents—approximately 9,100 original records—to
$`1\times 10^{-5}`$, then remains constant. We use Adam with
$`\beta_{1}=0.9`$, $`\beta_{2}=0.95`$, weight decay 0.1, and gradient
clipping at 1.0, while retaining two shared-weight MTP layers with an
auxiliary-loss scaling factor of 0.3.

### 3.2 Reinforcement Learning

Reinforcement learning improves the general capabilities of the SFT
model and provides a strong foundation for developing specialized
teacher models. At this stage, we jointly apply RLVR and RLHF, routing
samples from each environment to the corresponding verifier or reward
model for scoring. Compared with Nemotron 3 Ultra ([NVIDIA,
2026c](#bib.bib59)), we refresh the RL training data with more recent
collections and perform reward profiling before training. We use the
same training algorithm as [NVIDIA (2026c)](#bib.bib59), augmented with
two optimizations designed to mitigate train–inference mismatch: router
replay ([Ma et al., 2025](#bib.bib50)) and using FP32 for LM head ([Chen
et al., 2025](#bib.bib8)). Together, these optimizations reduce the KL
error between the training and inference engines by more than an order
of magnitude.

We also incorporate new data such as the competitive-programming RL
environment of [Ficek et al. (2026)](#bib.bib22), comprising 2,847
training problems and 372 validation problems from past Olympiad-level
competitions, each paired with a validated executable evaluator.
Generated C++ solutions are compiled and executed against
problem-specific tests, receiving a binary reward of 1 for full credit
and 0 otherwise.

To improve tool use and cross-document reasoning on financial tasks, we
built a finance-agent environment on top of NeMo Gym⁸⁸ 8
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/tree/main/resources_servers/finance_sec_search](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/tree/main/resources_servers/finance_sec_search),
targeting the capabilities measured by the Vals.ai Finance Agent
benchmark⁹⁹ 9
[https://www.vals.ai/benchmarks/finance_agent](https://www.vals.ai/benchmarks/finance_agent).
The environment provides the same tools—SEC filing search, document
parsing, evidence retrieval, and final source-supported
submission—during trajectory generation, supervised fine-tuning (SFT),
reinforcement learning (RL), and evaluation, avoiding format and tool
mismatch across stages. We use two synthetic data generation (SDG)
tracks. The document-grounded track samples windowed contexts from SEC
10-K, 10-Q, and 8-K filings of S&P 500 companies, generates
analyst-style questions with a teacher model, and verifies answerability
and answer quality using multi-seed voting and GenSelect ([Toshniwal et
al., 2025](#bib.bib80)). The agentic track targets gaps found in a
coverage audit against the Vals.ai category taxonomy by making the
relationship among source documents—reporting period, form type, and
company—an explicit sampling variable. Validated questions are re-solved
by teacher models in the same environment to produce verified tool-use
trajectories, and are routed to the SFT or RL pool according to their
measured difficulty.

To support training across a broad and diverse set of environments, we
use a global batch size of 8,192, with 16 rollouts generated per sample.
The maximum generation length is initially set to 80K tokens and later
increased to 128K.

### 3.3 Agentic and reasoning teacher model

#### 3.3.1 Agentic Teacher

We initialize the Agentic Teacher from the step-120 text-RLVR checkpoint
and continue training with full end to end GRPO on agentic tasks. We do
not use any single step environments during the training of the agentic
teacher, rather opting to train the end to end agentic teacher on
sandbox, fully executable agentic Terminal, SWE, and Search tasks.

We use 64 prompts and 16 generations per prompt, giving a total of 1024
batch size. We keep a max trajectory age of one for the training of the
agentic teacher. Each task was given a maximum timeout of 2 hours per
rollout. Tasks were profiled for difficulty for the step-120 text-RLVR
checkpoint.

#### 3.3.2 Reasoning teacher

We initialize the reasoning teacher from the step-120 text-RLVR
checkpoint and continue training with the same asynchronous GRPO
framework. The main differences are the training mixture and the
generation budget. We replace the broad RLVR mixture with a
reasoning-focused curriculum of 25,600 examples, with each 256-prompt
batch containing 90% science, 5% mathematics, and 5% competitive-coding
problems. The maximum generation length is set to 131,072 throughout
training.

We use 256 prompts per update and 16 responses per prompt, giving 4,096
total batch size. We increase the maximum trajectory age from one to two
updates compared to the initial RLVR setup. The learning rate remains
$`4\times 10^{-6}`$. Mathematics and STEM rewards use answer
verification with DeepSeek-V4-Flash¹⁰¹⁰ 10
https://huggingface.co/deepseek-ai/DeepSeek-V4-Flash as the LLM judge,
while coding rewards use execution-based tests. We select the checkpoint
after 80 updates as the reasoning teacher for the MOPD stage.

### 3.4 Vision teacher models

#### 3.4.1 Video reasoning and tracking teacher

We train a video teacher on a mixture of video-QA reasoning data which
is carefully curated from video training subset of Nemotron 3 Nano Omni
([NVIDIA, 2026a](#bib.bib57)) and SA-V object-tracking data ([Ravi et
al., 2024](#bib.bib68)). Video-QA examples contain multiple-choice
questions over 64 ordered frames, which are processed as a single video.
SA-V examples contain box, point, and referring-object tracking tasks
whose frames are processed as ordered multi-image inputs. This mixture
targets complementary high-level video-reasoning (e.g. temporal,
attribute, action and object) and fine-grained spatiotemporal-tracking
capabilities.

Both sources use deterministic, verifiable task rewards rather than an
LLM judge. Video-QA uses a binary reward based on multiple-choice answer
correctness. For SA-V, the model generates structured tracks over the
requested frames, and the verifier assigns a continuous reward based on
localization quality, object-visibility accuracy, and the validity and
completeness of the predicted track. Missing, invalid, or extraneous
track entries are penalized.

We additionally apply two auxiliary shaping signals. First, a response
without exactly one properly matched \<think\>...\</think\> block is
considered malformed: its task reward is set to zero and its training
advantage is overridden to $`-5`$. This prevents an otherwise correct
answer with malformed reasoning structure from being reinforced. Second,
we apply a soft length preference only to correct responses. Reasoning
up to 1,024 tokens retains the full task reward; between 1,024 and 4,096
tokens, the reward multiplier decreases linearly from $`1.0`$ to
$`0.95`$; and beyond 4,096 tokens it remains at $`0.95`$. This profile
band does not truncate generation and introduces at most a 5% preference
for concise, correct reasoning.

We initialize from the step-120 text-RLVR checkpoint and train using
asynchronous GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib73)) in NeMo-RL ([NVIDIA,
2025b](#bib.bib55)). Each update uses 128 prompts and 16 responses per
prompt, giving a global batch of 2,048 trajectories. We replay the MoE
expert-routing decisions recorded during rollout generation when
evaluating the policy, reducing train–generation log-probability
discrepancies. The MoE router and vision modules remain frozen. Training
uses a constant learning rate of $`3\times 10^{-6}`$ on 128 NVIDIA GB200
GPUs.

We select step 60 as the final teacher. It achieves 74.90 on Video-MME
under the 256-frame reasoning protocol ([Fu et al., 2025a](#bib.bib23)),
19.10 on Video-MME-v2 under the corresponding 256-frame reasoning
protocol ([Fu et al., 2026](#bib.bib24)), 52.59 on GOT-10k ([Huang et
al., 2021](#bib.bib32)), and 60.00 on Molmo2-VideoTrack ([Clark et al.,
2026](#bib.bib15)). This checkpoint is subsequently used as the
video-specialized teacher during MOPD.

#### 3.4.2 Vision tool use teacher

To promote effective tool use in complex vision tasks, we train a vision
tool-calling teacher on a curated mixture of internal data. The target
tools include image cropping, zooming, rotation, flipping, difference
detection, side-by-side comparison of images, overlays, object counting,
and color analysis, with all tool calls represented as structured XML.
The training set consists of synthetically generated multiple-choice and
numerical questions that require one or more of the target tools to
solve. We further filter the data by difficulty, retaining samples that
are nearly impossible to solve correctly without using the available
tools. We use outcome correctness as the primary reward, as well as an
auxiliary tool-use reward to penalize duplicate and malformed tool
calls. We initialize from the text-RLVR checkpoint and train using
asynchronous GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib73)) in NeMo-RL ([NVIDIA,
2025b](#bib.bib55)). The teacher checkpoint selection is guided by the
target vision tool use benchmark accuracy on V\* [Wu & Xie
(2024)](#bib.bib87), HRBench8K [Wang et al. (2025b)](#bib.bib83), and
BabyVision [Chen et al. (2026b)](#bib.bib10)..

#### 3.4.3 Vision reasoning RLVR

To improve reasoning over visual inputs, we train on a curated mixture
of chart understanding, document and table reasoning, OCR, geometry, and
visual reasoning tasks. The first stage draws from 16,090 profiled
examples. The second stage uses a mixture of 13,848 examples, comprising
8,366 chart-reasoning questions from Chart datasets, together with 5,482
examples emphasizing document, table, and OCR understanding. We audit
reference-answer formats and compatibility with the reward functions,
and remove exact question–answer duplicates from the second-stage
mixture. We use outcome correctness as the training reward, evaluated
using task-appropriate mathematical, multiple-choice, and
string-matching verifiers.

We initialize from an existing multimodal RLVR checkpoint and train
using asynchronous GRPO ([Shao et al., 2024](#bib.bib73)) in NeMo-RL
([NVIDIA, 2025b](#bib.bib55)), with 256 prompts per update and 16
sampled responses per prompt. The second stage initializes from the
first-stage checkpoint at step 114 with a fresh optimizer. We use the
checkpoint obtained after 20 additional updates on the second-stage
mixture. Both stages use 128 GPUs distributed across 32 compute nodes.

### 3.5 Alignment teacher

We train an alignment teacher that has superior chat capabilities and is
well calibrated with human preferences. We train this teacher primarily
using RLHF. First, we train a new variant of the Nemotron 3 Ultra GenRM
that is trained with a reasoning length penalty to be more token
efficient. Combined with MTP inference, it enables substantially faster
training. We then conduct policy training in a similar manner to
Nemotron 3 Ultra. However, rather than doing self-comparison in a
cyclical format, we instead compare all policy responses to a fixed
response from a strong open-source model, Gemma 4 31B-it. We also use
the mean of per rubric scores to evaluate responses instead of an
overall score. Our training blend is similar to Nemotron 3 Ultra but
includes newly collected preference data, updated model identity and
capabilities data, and has more emphasis on addressing language mixing.
We mix this RLHF training with the abstention training strategy from
Nemotron 3 Ultra and use a small amount of reasoning length penalty. We
evaluate downstream policy improvements on our internal chat benchmark.

### 3.6 MOPD

Nemotron 3.5 Super included a multi-teacher on-policy distillation
(MOPD) stage after RL which distills the capabilities of the specialized
teachers of the preceding subsections into a single student model
similar to the development of Nemotron 3 Ultra ([NVIDIA,
2026c](#bib.bib59)); the final MTP boosting phase (Section
[3.7](#S3.SS7 "3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
then runs on the MOPD output. Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, however did not
have an MOPD stage. In this section we detail the MOPD process for
Nemotron 3.5 Super. The student is the RL checkpoint of Section
[3.2](#S3.SS2 "3.2 Reinforcement Learning ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
after 120 steps, the same checkpoint the teachers were branched from.
Given a prompt from some domain, the student generates a response; for
each token $`x`$ with context $`c`$ (the prompt and preceding tokens),
the teacher that owns the domain is consulted for its probability
$`p_{T}(x\mid c)`$, and the student is updated to raise the probability
of tokens the teacher rates higher than it does and to lower it for
tokens the teacher rates lower. Concretely, let $`\pi_{S}`$ be the
student with parameters $`\theta`$, $`\pi_{T}`$ the domain teacher, and
$`c_{t}`$ the context of token $`y_{t}`$ in the student’s response
$`y`$. The student minimizes the reverse KL
$`D_{\mathrm{KL}}(\pi_{S}(\cdot|c_{t})\,\|\,\pi_{T}(\cdot|c_{t}))`$ at
every position of its own responses, using the token-local update ([Lu &
Lab, 2025](#bib.bib49); [Yang et al., 2026](#bib.bib96); [Xiao et al.,
2026](#bib.bib88)) and treating the sampled contexts as fixed during
each update,

|     |                                    |                                                              |                                                                    |                   |
| --- | ---------------------------------- | ------------------------------------------------------------ | ------------------------------------------------------------------ | ----------------- | -------------------------- | --- | --- |
|     | $`\displaystyle\Delta\theta\;`$    | $`\displaystyle\propto\;\mathbb{E}_{y\sim\bar{\pi}_{S}(\cdot | q)}\Big[\sum*{t}\widehat{A}*{t}\,\nabla*{\theta}\log\pi*{S}(y\_{t} | c\_{t})\Big]\Big  | \_{\theta=\bar{\theta}},`$ |     | (1) |
|     | $`\displaystyle\widehat{A}_{t}\;`$ | $`\displaystyle=\;\mathrm{sg}\!\left[\log\pi*{T}(y*{t}       | c*{t})-\log\bar{\pi}*{S}(y\_{t}                                    | c\_{t})\right],`$ |                            | (2) |

where $`\bar{\pi}_{S}`$ is the frozen snapshot of the student taken
before the update (parameters $`\bar{\theta}`$), $`\mathrm{sg}[\cdot]`$
denotes stop-gradient, and the expectation is over fresh responses
sampled from $`\bar{\pi}_{S}`$ with the update evaluated at
$`\theta=\bar{\theta}`$. Every generated token receives a dense
advantage from a single teacher forward pass, with no verifier and no
sampling from the teacher; the direct contribution is zero where student
and teacher agree, so the signal concentrates where the student departs
from the specialist. In practice training is asynchronous and responses
may come from the previous snapshot $`\bar{\pi}_{S-1}`$, i.e. one step
stale; following the Ultra recipe, tokens are then importance-weighted
by $`\bar{\pi}_{S}/\bar{\pi}_{S-1}`$ and those with weights outside
$`[0.2,5]`$ are masked ([Team et al., 2025](#bib.bib78)). These
token-level weights correct action probabilities at the sampled
contexts; context-distribution mismatch and masking introduce bias. Only
student-generated tokens enter the loss.

MOPD for Nemotron 3.5 Super is run in two stages on 128 GB200 nodes. We
found that benchmarks improve at different rates under distillation, and
that some peak within a few steps and then decline with further
training. To mitigate this, stage 1 is weighted toward the domains that
improve quickly and tend to regress later, and stage 2 toward the
domains that improve more slowly. Prompts come from the environments
used to train each teacher and are routed to that teacher; environments
no specialist owns are routed to a _self-anchor_, a frozen copy of the
student’s starting checkpoint, which provides no signal while the
student still matches it and regularizes departures from it.

##### Stage 1.

The student is the RL checkpoint. It is distilled from the agentic,
reasoning, alignment (RLHF) and vision tool-use teachers with prompt
shares of 35%, 25%, 20% and 15%, plus 5% routed to the RL checkpoint as
self-anchor. Batches contain 512 prompts with one rollout each,
generation is capped at 48K tokens in a 128K context, and the learning
rate is $`2\times 10^{-6}`$. We take the checkpoint after 30 steps as
the stage-1 model.

##### Stage 2.

Stage 2 targets chat quality (e.g. LMArena-style evaluations) and the
vision domains, which improved more slowly in stage 1 than the agentic
and reasoning benchmarks. The stage-1 model is the student and
self-anchor. The agentic and reasoning teachers are retired and their
environments folded into the self-anchor stream, so the student is
regularized toward the stage-1 model on them rather than distilled
again. Four specialists are distilled: the alignment teacher, the vision
tool-use teacher, a vision-reasoning teacher, and the video reasoning
and tracking teacher of Section
[3.4.1](#S3.SS4.SSS1 "3.4.1 Video reasoning and tracking teacher ‣ 3.4 Vision teacher models ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
with shares of 30%, 20%, 20% and 20%, plus 10% self-anchor. Batches grow
to 1,024 prompts, generation may use the full 128K context, the learning
rate is $`3\times 10^{-6}`$, and the stage runs for 40 steps, one pass
over its prompts.

In stage 2 the distillation target is smoothed with the proximal-teacher
construction of trust-region policy distillation ([Xie et al.,
2026](#bib.bib91)) (see also [Xing et al. (2026)](#bib.bib92)): the
teacher in Equation
[2](#S3.E2 "In 3.6 MOPD ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
is replaced by the frozen mixture
$`\alpha\,\pi_{T}+(1-\alpha)\,\bar{\pi}_{S}`$ with $`\alpha=0.2`$. This
bounds the advantage below by $`\log(1-\alpha)\approx-0.22`$, so tokens
the teacher considers very unlikely receive a bounded negative signal
rather than an unbounded one, which stabilizes training once the student
has already absorbed one round of teachers. Stage 1 uses $`\alpha=1`$.

Teachers run as independent, non-colocated inference fleets (tensor
parallel 8, expert parallel 8) sized to each domain’s share and sequence
length, and score trajectories as they arrive, so rollout generation,
teacher scoring and student optimization form a pipeline in NeMo-RL
([NVIDIA, 2025b](#bib.bib55)). The MTP heads are trained jointly with
the language model throughout (Section
[3.7](#S3.SS7 "3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).

### 3.7 MTP Boosting

We apply a dedicated MTP-only training recipe at two points: first as a
short warm start from the Nemotron 3.5 Super SFT checkpoint, and again
after all backbone post-training stages are complete. We refer to the
latter phase as _MTP boosting_. For Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, we skip the
warm start and apply only MTP boosting. In both runs, the backbone
remains frozen and only the MTP head parameters are updated.

The recipe has two complementary components. First, we continue applying
the hidden-state-mixing strategy described in Section
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
This further exposes the MTP head to inference-like conditions, reducing
the train–inference mismatch and improving acceptance at longer draft
lengths. Second, whereas the preceding MTP training stages use
cross-entropy loss, these runs use a knowledge-distillation loss with
the frozen backbone as the teacher. This teacher-distribution
supervision improves alignment between the MTP head and the backbone and
is particularly beneficial under non-greedy sampling.

In order to measure MTP draft quality we report evaluation results on
the SPEED-Bench ([Abramovich et al., 2026b](#bib.bib2)) qualitative
split using per-sample acceptance length (AL).

##### Data.

We generate on-policy rollouts using the corresponding backbone
checkpoint. Seed prompts are drawn from
Nemotron-Post-Training-Dataset-v2¹¹¹¹ 11
[https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-Post-Training-Dataset-v2](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-Post-Training-Dataset-v2)
and Nemotron-RL-Super-Training-Blends¹²¹² 12
[https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-RL-Super-Training-Blends](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-RL-Super-Training-Blends)
to cover general-purpose and agentic inputs, respectively. We sample
rollouts at $`temp=1`$, cap sequences at 8K tokens, and accumulate the
loss only over assistant-response tokens.

##### Training Procedure.

We first train the MTP head for $`4k`$ steps from the Nemotron 3.5 Super
SFT checkpoint. This intermediate run plateaus after substantially fewer
training iterations than the final-checkpoint run. When transferred to
the final post-trained checkpoint, the resulting MTP head retains
significant part of its acceptance-length improvement, as shown in Table
[8](#S3.T8 "Table 8 ‣ Training Procedure. ‣ 3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
We therefore use the SFT-trained MTP head to initialize the final MTP
boosting phase and train it for an additional $`25k`$ steps against the
final Nemotron 3.5 Super backbone. For Nemotron 3.5 Lightining, we ran
MTP boosting for $`48k`$ steps against the final backbone. Whereas
Nemotron 3 Ultra uses a temperature-scaled forward-KL objective, we now
use the LK loss ([Samarin et al., 2026](#bib.bib72)).

| \+ 4k MTP-only training iterations  | 5.080      | 4.730      |
| Nemotron 3.5 Super (Final)          | 4.287      | 3.787      |
| \+ MTP head from the SFT-stage run  | 4.528      | 4.436      |

##### Nemotron 3.5 Super Results.

The Boosted-MTP head improves over the original MTP at every draft
position (Figure
[6](#S3.F6 "Figure 6 ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super Results. ‣ 3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
with the largest gains at deeper positions, where the train–inference
mismatch is most severe: at inference, these positions condition on the
head’s own hidden states rather than on the backbone’s. These
per-position gains translate into higher AL in every SPEED-Bench
category (Table
[9](#S3.T9 "Table 9 ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super Results. ‣ 3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
raising the average AL at $`T=1`$ from 3.78 to 4.61 accepted tokens.
