#### 3.8.1 Evaluation Setup

We evaluate Nemotron 3.5 Super on a comprehensive suite of benchmarks
spanning agentic capabilities, reasoning and knowledge, multimodal,
conversational ability and instruction following, long-context
understanding, and multilingual performance. All evaluation results for
Nemotron 3.5 Super and baselines were collected via Nemo Gym¹³¹³ 13
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym).
For reproducibility purposes, more details on the evaluation settings
can be found in the Nemo Gym recipes folder and the corresponding
reproducibility tutorial¹⁴¹⁴ 14
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/super-3.5/reproducibility.md](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/super-3.5/reproducibility.md).

The following benchmarks are not onboarded yet in our open source tools
and for these we used either their official open source implementation
or otherwise an internal scaffolding that we plan to open source in the
future: BabyVision, CharXiv-RQ, MathVision, MMLongBench-Doc,
OCR-Reasoning, OCRBench-v2, V\*, Video-MME-v2. NV Chat Benchmark is an
internal chat benchmark developed at NVIDIA.

All models were evaluated under the same evaluation settings including
agentic resources (CPU compute and timeouts, tools, search APIs), input
data, prompt templates, number of repeats, and extraction/metric
implementation. Inference parameters such as temperature and top_p, and
maximum number of tokens were directly extracted from the
recommendations of the corresponding model cards.

##### Agentic Capabilities.

We evaluate agentic capabilities using SWE-bench Verified ([Jimenez et
al., 2023](#bib.bib37)), SWE-bench Multilingual ([Yang et al.,
2025](#bib.bib94)), and SWE-bench Pro ([Deng et al., 2026](#bib.bib20))
for software engineering; Terminal-Bench 2.1 ([Merrill et al.,
2026](#bib.bib52)) for terminal-based task execution; the Retail,
Airline, and Telecom domains of $`\tau^{2}`$-Bench ([Barres et al.,
2025](#bib.bib5)) and the Banking domain of $`\tau^{3}`$-Bench ([Shi et
al., 2026](#bib.bib75)) for conversational tool use; GDPVal ([Patwardhan
et al., 2025](#bib.bib63)) for economically valuable real-world
workplace tasks; and BrowseComp ([Wei et al., 2025](#bib.bib85)) for web
browsing and information seeking. All SWE-bench variants run in the
OpenCode harness¹⁵¹⁵ 15
[https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode](https://github.com/anomalyco/opencode)
inside a sandbox with web search and web fetch disabled, and we report
the resolve rate averaged over three runs. Terminal-Bench 2.1 uses the
Terminus 2 harness and is averaged over eight runs. All $`\tau`$-Bench
domains are averaged over eight trials. The $`\tau^{2}`$-Bench domains
use Qwen3-235B-A22B-Instruct-2507 as the user simulator; in the Banking
domain the agent retrieves from the bank’s knowledge base with BM25 and
grep tools, and GPT-5.4-mini acts as the user simulator.

Our open-source implementation of GDPVal loosely follows the AA v2.1
methodology using the same sandboxing environment with the respect to
python and system packages, and a Stirrup agent. The reference committee
includes a subset of the models from the AA leaderboard for which the
reference files were collected independently (DeepSeek V4 Flash 0731,
DeepSeek V4 Pro, Inkling Small, GLM-5.1, Kimi K2.6, Nemotron 3 Ultra,
Qwen3.6-35B-A3B, Qwen3.5-122B-A10B, Qwen3.5-397B-A17B, GPT-OSS-120b,
Gemma 4 26B-A4B, Qwen3-30B-A3B-Thinking-2507). The judge committee is
the same as the one described in AA methodology (GPT-5.6 Sol, medium
reasoning, Gemini 3.8 Flash, high reasoning, and Claude Opus 5, high
effort).

BrowseComp lacks a standardized harness, and scores vary substantially
with search provider, browsing tools, context management, and step
budget. We therefore evaluate all models on a single open-source harness
released in NeMo Gym. The harness provides search and browse tools
backed by Exa¹⁶¹⁶ 16 [https://exa.ai](https://exa.ai) (deep mode) and
stores retrieved pages in a per-sample workspace accessible through a
read-only shell tool. Context is managed with a discard-all policy: when
the conversation exceeds a token threshold, prior turns are dropped and
the model maintains progress across resets via a memory tool. We
additionally report BrowseComp on an OpenCode-based harness (with minor
tool-configuration changes) under identical serving settings for all
models, and observe that Nemotron 3.5 Super’s performance transfers to
this second harness as well.

##### Code Generation.

We evaluate code generation using LiveCodeBench v6 ([Jain et al.,
2024](#bib.bib36)) for competitive programming and SciCode ([Tian et
al., 2024](#bib.bib79)) for scientific research coding. For
LiveCodeBench, we use the 454 problems released between August 2024 and
May 2025, prompt the model to solve them without tools, and report
pass@1 averaged over three samples. SciCode contains 65 research
problems written by scientists and decomposed into 288 subproblems; we
include the scientist-provided background in the prompt and average over
eight repeats. We report both subtask accuracy, the fraction of
subproblems whose generated code passes its tests, and problem accuracy,
the fraction of problems for which all subproblems pass.

##### Math and Science.

We evaluate mathematical and scientific reasoning using GPQA-Diamond
([Rein et al., 2023](#bib.bib69)) for graduate-level science questions;
Humanity’s Last Exam (HLE) ([Phan et al., 2025](#bib.bib64)) for
expert-level questions at the frontier of human knowledge; MathArena
Apex-Shortlist ([Dekoninck et al., 2026](#bib.bib19)) for competition
mathematics; CritPt ([Zhu et al., 2025](#bib.bib99)) for research-level
physics reasoning; and AA-Omniscience ([Jackson et al.,
2025](#bib.bib35)) for factual knowledge and hallucination resistance.
GPQA-Diamond is averaged over eight repeats and Apex-Shortlist over 16.
Apex-Shortlist answers are checked by symbolic equivalence, and the
model has no tool access. For HLE we report the 2,158 text-only
questions here, both without tools and with tools. Without tools,
answers are graded by GPT-5.6 Luna using the official HLE judge prompt.
In the with-tools setting, Nemotron 3.5 Super can run Python code and
search the web; this result comes from a separate internal harness.
CritPt is scored with the official Artificial Analysis grading API,
using five samples for each of its 70 problems. For AA-Omniscience, we
use the 600-question public set, average over ten repeats, and grade
with GPT-5.6 Luna. We report accuracy and the non-hallucination rate.

##### Multimodal.

We evaluate multimodal understanding across visual reasoning, charts,
documents and OCR, video, and computer use. We use BabyVision ([Chen et
al., 2026b](#bib.bib10)) for basic visual reasoning; CharXiv reasoning
questions ([Wang et al., 2024b](#bib.bib84)) for scientific chart
understanding; MathVision ([Wang et al., 2024a](#bib.bib81)) for
mathematical reasoning in visual contexts; OCR-Reasoning ([Huang et al.,
2026](#bib.bib33)) and OCRBench v2 ([Fu et al., 2025b](#bib.bib25)), the
latter with separate English and Chinese scores, for text-rich image
understanding; V\* ([Wu & Xie, 2024](#bib.bib87)) for fine-grained
visual search in high-resolution images; Video-MME-v2 ([Fu et al.,
2026](#bib.bib24)) for video understanding; MMLongBench-Doc ([Ma et al.,
2024](#bib.bib51)) for long multi-page document understanding; the full
HLE set ([Phan et al., 2025](#bib.bib64)), including image-based
questions; and OSWorld-Verified ([Xie et al., 2024](#bib.bib89); [Xie et
al., 2025](#bib.bib90)) for computer-use agents. Unless noted otherwise,
results are averaged over three repeats. Free-form answers are extracted
or graded by an LLM judge: GPT-4o-mini for BabyVision, MathVision, and
OCR-Reasoning, Claude Opus 4.7 for CharXiv, GPT-4o for MMLongBench-Doc,
and GPT-3.5-Turbo for answer extraction on V\*. OCRBench v2 and
Video-MME-v2 use rule-based scoring. V\* is evaluated as static multiple
choice without a visual search tool. Video-MME-v2 is evaluated without
subtitles. MMLongBench-Doc provides up to 100 rendered pages per
document, one image per page. HLE (full) covers all 2,500 questions with
a single sample, graded by GPT-5.6 Luna. For OSWorld-Verified, we run
the 361 tasks that do not require Google Drive access on cloud-hosted
desktop virtual machines. The agent observes screenshots only, acts
through pyautogui commands, and has up to 100 steps per task. We report
the average task score, which includes partial credit, over three runs.

##### Long Context.

We evaluate long-context understanding with AA-LCR ([Artificial Analysis
Team, 2025](#bib.bib3)) and GraphWalks ([OpenAI, 2025](#bib.bib62)).
AA-LCR (v1.1) contains 100 questions that require reasoning across sets
of real-world documents totaling about 100K tokens. We average over 16
repeats and grade answers with GPT-5.6 Luna following the official
protocol. GraphWalks gives the model a graph as an edge list in the
prompt and asks for the result of a breadth-first search or for the
parents of a node. We include both task types across all context-length
buckets and keep the 950 of 1,150 prompts that fit within 1M tokens; all
models are scored on the same 950 prompts. The score is the F1 between
the predicted and reference node sets, from a single sample.

##### Instruction Following and Chat Preference.

We assess instruction following with IFBench ([Pyatkin et al.,
2025](#bib.bib65)), which measures how well models generalize to 58 new,
out-of-domain verifiable output constraints. We report the fraction of
constraints satisfied per prompt under strict verification, averaged
over prompts and eight samples. NV Chat Benchmark measures chat
preference as a win rate in pairwise comparisons. It covers 3,713
prompts, and each prompt has an opponent response drawn from
human-preference battles. Gemini 3.1 Pro judges each pair in both
presentation orders, with style control based on response length. In
addition, Table
[11](#S3.T11 "Table 11 ‣ Instruction Following and Chat Preference. ‣ 3.8.1 Evaluation Setup ‣ 3.8 Post-trained Model Evaluations ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
reports Text Arena ([Chiang et al., 2024](#bib.bib13); [Li et al.,
2024](#bib.bib45)) Elo ratings derived from human pairwise preference

##### Multilingual.

We evaluate multilingual capabilities using MMLU-ProX ([Xuan et al.,
2025](#bib.bib93)) for multilingual knowledge and reasoning and WMT24++
([Deutsch et al., 2025](#bib.bib21)) for machine translation. For
MMLU-ProX, we report accuracy averaged over ten languages: English,
German, Spanish, French, Italian, Japanese, Chinese, Hindi, Portuguese,
and Korean. For WMT24++, we translate from English into the nine
non-English languages of this set and report reference-based XCOMET-XXL
([Guerreiro et al., 2024](#bib.bib29)) scores, macro-averaged over
language pairs.

### 3.9 Reasoning Efficiency and Control

Nemotron 3.5 Super is trained for three reasoning modes: reasoning-off,
regular and medium-effort. The regular and medium-effort reasoning modes
have the option to be used in conjunction with inference-time budget
control. These combinations of controls provide flexibilities that cover
the entire spectrum of accuracy-efficiency trade-off to meet customers’
needs in various application scenarios.

The medium-effort reasoning mode is introduced during the SFT stage,
with data generated by Kimi K3’s low-effort mode, and later optimized
during the RL stage. Following the same recipe as Nemotron 3 Ultra, the
reasoning traces of a small fraction of SFT data are truncated to random
reasoning budgets while the responses remain the same and with
\</think\> tokens masked from training loss, to prepare the model for
inference-time budget control. Approximately 4% of the RL training
prompts are in the medium-effort mode and they cover math, STEM and
coding, and length-based adjustments are applied on the RL rewards for
them. This is the same recipe that has trained Nemotron 3 Ultra and
Super and the effects of effort-level optimization generalize to a
variety of tasks beyond math, STEM and coding. Nemotron 3.5 Super’s
medium-effort mode uses around 2.2X less tokens than the regular mode,
averaged over the benchmarks in Artificial Analysis Intelligence Index,
at the cost of approximately 9% drop in accuracy.

Figure
[7](#S3.F7 "Figure 7 ‣ 3.9 Reasoning Efficiency and Control ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares Nemotron 3.5 Super against similar-sized models on SWE-bench
Pro and demonstrates substantial efficiency advantage in GPU-hours.

![Refer to
caption](2610.38095v1/figures/accuracy_vs_gpu_time_swebenchpro.png)

Figure 7: Accuracy–compute trade-off on SWE-bench Pro. We evaluate each
model, record the resulting agent trajectories, and use the captured
traces to benchmark task throughput (tasks per hour). The vertical axis
reports Pass@1 accuracy averaged over three evaluation runs. The
horizontal axis reports the aggregate GPU time, in GPU-hours, required
to complete 2,193 tasks (three repeats of the public set). The compute
footprint is four NVIDIA B200 GPUs. All models use NVFP4 precision
except DeepSeek V4 Flash 0731, which uses MXFP4.

It is important for the model to not "overfit" into one single harness.
We evaluate Nemotron 3.5 Super across six agent harnesses on SWE-bench
Pro and Terminal-Bench 2.1. Figure

compares the observed scores under each harness’s standard

and its configuration alongside model results.

![Refer to caption](2610.38095v1/figures/multiharness.png)

Figure 8: Nemotron 3.5 Super performance across six agent harnesses on

### 3.11 Test-Time Scaling through Parallel Agents and Self-Verification

We further improve the final model’s agentic performance through
parallel agents followed by self-verification. For each task, $`N`$
independent instances of the same post-trained agent attempt the task,
producing a pool of candidate trajectories. These agents can execute in
parallel without inter-agent communication. The same checkpoint then
serves as a verifier, selecting one candidate using LLM-as-a-Verifier
(LAV) [Kwok et al. (2026)](#bib.bib41). This requires neither additional
parameter updates nor a separately trained verifier.

To illustrate the selection process, consider Terminal-Bench 2.1, where
each task has five candidate trajectories. The verifier receives the
task description and a pair of trajectories, and evaluates them against
three criteria: specification adherence, output match, and error signal
detection. These criteria assess whether the agent meets the task’s
explicit requirements, whether its observed terminal output supports
successful completion, and whether unresolved errors remain. Each
evaluation focuses on one criterion and assigns a separate grade
distribution to each trajectory. Following LAV, we use a 20-level
grading scale represented by single-token labels A–T, ordered from best
to worst. We compute each trajectory’s score as the probability-weighted
average over these grades, normalized to $`[0,1]`$, rather than
selecting a single discrete grade.

For each compared pair and criterion, we evaluate both presentation
orders (once with trajectory $`i`$ first and $`j`$ second, and once with
their positions reversed; $`K=2`$). We align the resulting scores with
the original candidate identities and average across both orders and all
three criteria to mitigate presentation-order bias and evaluation noise.
Each compared pair therefore receives six evaluations of the same two
trajectories, without generating additional agent rollouts. The
resulting pair-specific scores $`R_{i}`$ and $`R_{j}`$ are converted
into a preference probability using the Bradley–Terry model
$`p_{ij}=1/(1+\exp(-(R_{i}-R_{j})))`$ [Bradley & Terry
(1952)](#bib.bib7).

These pairwise evaluations are organized using LAV’s Probabilistic Pivot
Tournament (PPT). For the five Terminal-Bench candidates, the tournament
first compares neighboring candidates in a random ring, for example
$`(1,2)`$, $`(2,3)`$, $`(3,4)`$, $`(4,5)`$, and $`(5,1)`$. The two
candidates with the highest mean preference across their ring
comparisons become pivots. Each of the remaining three candidates is
then compared against both pivots, and the two pivots are compared with
each other; cached evaluations are reused for identical directed
comparisons. Finally, the preferences from both stages are aggregated,
and the candidate with the highest mean preference across its
comparisons is selected. Normalizing by the number of comparisons
accounts for the pivots participating in more comparisons, and any of
the five candidates can ultimately win.

We apply the same procedure across all six benchmarks, using five
candidate trajectories for Terminal-Bench 2.1 and DeepSWE, and three for
SWE-bench Verified, Pro, Multilingual, and BrowseComp. All benchmarks
use three criteria and $`K=2`$. Terminal-Bench and the three SWE-bench
variants use two pivots, while DeepSWE and BrowseComp use one pivot. For
BrowseComp, all reported metrics use the 400-problem subset used for our
internal evaluation tracking, given the cost of evaluating the full
benchmark.

As shown in Fig.
[9](#S3.F9 "Figure 9 ‣ 3.11 Test-Time Scaling through Parallel Agents and Self-Verification ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
selection improves observed accuracy across all six benchmarks.
Terminal-Bench accuracy increases from 62.7% to 71.9%, DeepSWE from
24.6% to 34.5%, and BrowseComp from 71.5% to 79.8%—absolute gains of
9.2, 9.9, and 8.3 percentage points, respectively. SWE-bench Verified,
Pro, and Multilingual improve by 1.1, 2.4, and 1.9 percentage points. On
the BrowseComp subset, selection recovers approximately 70% of the gap
between pass@1 and oracle best-of-three performance. These results
demonstrate an inference-time path to higher task accuracy that
complements post-training without changing model parameters.

![Refer to caption](2610.38095v1/figures/test-time-scaling.png)

Figure 9: Test-time scaling with the final post-trained checkpoint. Blue
bars show empirical pass@1, averaged over the candidate rollouts; green
segments show the improvement from LAV selection, with total bar height
indicating selected accuracy. Candidate-pool sizes are shown below each
benchmark. BrowseComp is evaluated on a 400-problem subset used for
internal evaluation tracking.

### 3.12 Benchmark Decontamination

Post-training data drawn from many synthetic and curated sources can
absorb benchmark content, not only as verbatim copies but also as
paraphrases, translations, augmentations, and other derived samples that
string matching misses ([Yang et al., 2023](#bib.bib95)). Such
contamination inflates benchmark scores and, because the same benchmarks
guide training monitoring and checkpoint selection, it can also result
in a worse final model. We therefore performed checks of post-training
data against 30 benchmarks (see the complete list in Appendix
[C.2](#A3.SS2 "C.2 Benchmarks ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
and removed samples related to benchmark questions. This data-side
detection complements model-side detection with CoDeC ([Zawalski et al.,
2026](#bib.bib97)), which probes a trained model for traces of a
benchmark. The pipeline will be released as part of NeMo Curator.

##### Decontamination pipeline.

The pipeline combines two simple similarity signals, which select
candidate matches with high recall, with an LLM judge, which verifies
each candidate precisely. Every training sample passes through four
stages:

1.  1.  Normalization. The sample is reduced to its problem statement, the
        part into which a benchmark question could leak. Text templates that
        recur across its dataset are stripped to avoid spurious matches.
2.  2.  Candidate retrieval. The sample is compared with every benchmark
        question using two signals. _Verbatim overlap_ measures how many
        $`n`$-grams of the question’s text appear in the sample. It detects
        verbatim copies, even inside longer texts, but misses any
        rephrasing. _Semantic overlap_ measures the embedding similarity
        between parts of the two texts. It effectively captures paraphrases,
        but can be biased by style similarity. Both signals are used to
        shortlist the most likely contamination candidates.
3.  3.  Verification. Each shortlisted pair of a sample and a benchmark
        question is verified by an LLM judge. The judge classifies the
        relationship either as contamination (direct copy, paraphrase,
        answer leakage, or method leakage) or as benign (style overlap or no
        contamination), and decides its severity. Because the shortlist is
        small, even a strong judge can be used in this step.
4.  4.  Removal. A sample is removed if the judge finds it severely
        contaminated with respect to any benchmark question.

##### Pipeline quality and results.

We evaluated the quality of the pipeline on synthetic contamination. For
this purpose, we generated over 10,000 variants of Terminal-Bench 2.1
and GPQA Diamond questions, produced by 13 augmentations of varying
strength. Those augmentations ranged from character noise and cropping
to LLM-generated paraphrases, translations, and abstractions. The
generated variants were mixed with a similar number of same-domain
distractors. The judge correctly identified 87.5% and 79.1% of the
contaminated samples, respectively, while flagging only a single
distractor as contaminated. More details on the evaluation setup can be
found in Appendix
[C.4](#A3.SS4 "C.4 Synthetic Evaluation ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

From the post-training data, the pipeline removed 20,671 samples (around
0.09%; 14,793 SFT and 5,878 RL samples), matching 1,808 benchmark
questions (6.0%) in 22 of the 30 benchmarks. Of the candidate pairs the
judge worked on, around 3% were flagged as severe contamination. See
Appendix
[C.5](#A3.SS5 "C.5 Notable Cases ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
for examples.

## 4 Quantization

We apply post-training quantization (PTQ) and quantization-aware
distillation (QAD) with ModelOpt¹⁷¹⁷ 17
[https://github.com/NVIDIA/Model-Optimizer](https://github.com/NVIDIA/Model-Optimizer)
on Lightning 3.5 and Super 3.5 VL to create a new efficiency-accuracy
frontier relative to the unquantized, post-trained BF16 checkpoint.

Recipe development starts by defining bookends: conservative and
aggressive. Some models might also define a very aggressive variant.
These bookends are used to project runtime efficiency gains at various
SLAs in the throughput-interactivity Pareto. Unless otherwise noted, we
assume 51200, 2048, and 1024 as the pre-cached ISL, new ISL, and OSL,
respectively. We measure speedups between a quantized candidate and BF16
using two SLAs:

- •
  Throughput (tok/s/gpu) at an SLA of 50 tok/s/usr
- •
  Max throughput (e.g., usually highest concurrency)

The deployment use case also influences recipe selection. For example,
Lightning 3.5 is designed to excel in local, agentic use cases as well
as datacenter deployments. We therefore quantize Lightning 3.5 to
minimize weight fetch and raise activation precision to maintain
accuracy. Lightning 3.5 deploys to W4A16 regardless of GPU SKU, while
Super 3.5 VL’s NVFP4 checkpoint deploys W4A4 on Blackwell, and W4A16 on
non-Blackwell platforms.

Quantizing the KV and Mamba SSM caches reduces HBM footprint and
improves decode throughput by minimizing data transfer. The KV cache
grows linearly with sequence length, whereas the Mamba SSM state is
constant in size. New to Super 3.5 VL is ablations on quantized NVFP4 KV
cache. We show in
§[4.5](#S4.SS5 "4.5 KV Cache Quantization Evaluations ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
that quantization of KV cache to NVFP4 in combination with quantized SSM
meets acceptance criteria.

Finally, we aim for the highest efficiency and lowest HBM footprint
while satisfying the following accuracy criteria:

- •
  Median accuracy recovery of 99% or higher, defined as the median of
  the ratios of NVFP4 to BF16 eval scores across several repeats.
- •
  Verbosity increase of no more than 5% relative to BF16.

Evaluation results for quantized Lightning 3.5 and Super 3.5 VL are
shown in
§[4.9](#S4.SS9 "4.9 Lightning 3.5 Evaluation and Results ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
§[4.10](#S4.SS10 "4.10 Super 3.5 VL Evaluation and Results ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
respectively.

### 4.1 Recipe Design

We explored three predefined precision recipes (Table
[12](#S4.T12 "Table 12 ‣ 4.1 Recipe Design ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
The released checkpoint uses the _aggressive_ recipe, with NVFP4 MoE and
shared-expert GEMMs and FP8 attention projections and Mamba linear
layers. AutoQuant was explored as an alternative sensitivity- driven
mixed-precision approach.

|                             |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| --------------------------- | ----------- | -------- | -------------------- | ---------- | --------------- |
|                             | _Reference_ |          | _Predefined recipes_ |            |                 |
| Layer / axis                | BF16        | FP8      | Conservative         | Aggressive | Very aggressive |
| _Vision path_               |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| C-RADIO encoder             | BF16        | BF16     | BF16                 | BF16       | BF16            |
| VL connector                | BF16        | BF16     | BF16                 | BF16       | BF16            |
| _Latent MoE_                |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| MoE + shared GEMM           | BF16        | FP8      | NVFP4^(a)            | NVFP4      | NVFP4           |
| Latent down/up proj         | BF16        | FP8      | BF16                 | BF16       | NVFP4           |
| Router / gate (compute)^(b) | FP32        | FP32     | FP32                 | FP32       | FP32            |
| _Attention_                 |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| QKV / out proj              | BF16        | FP8      | BF16                 | FP8        | NVFP4           |
| KV cache                    | BF16        | FP8      | FP8                  | FP8        | FP4             |
| BMM1 ($`QK^{\top}`$)        | BF16        | FP8      | FP8                  | FP8        | FP4             |
| BMM2 ($`PV`$)               | BF16        | FP8      | FP8                  | FP8        | FP8             |
| _Mamba_                     |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| Mamba linear                | BF16        | FP8      | BF16                 | FP8        | NVFP4           |
| Mamba SSM cache             | FP32        | FP32     | FP32                 | FP16+SR    | FP16+SR         |
| Mamba convolution           | BF16        | BF16     | BF16                 | BF16       | BF16            |
| _Embed / head_              |             |          |                      |            |                 |
| Embedding                   | BF16        | BF16     | BF16                 | BF16       | BF16            |
| lm_head                     | BF16        | FP8      | BF16                 | FP8        | W4A16           |
| Avg. BPE                    | 16.00^(c)   | 8.10^(c) | 5.15^(c)             | 4.82       | 4.64^(c)        |

^(a) FP8 considered for the shared expert during exploration; the
Conservative BPE estimate assumes NVFP4 for all shared experts. ^(b)
FP32 denotes router computation, not checkpoint storage; BPE uses
BF16-stored router weights in every column. ^(c) Estimated by applying
each column’s weight policy to corresponding backbone and MTP modules;
MTP-specific fusion projection and normalization parameters remain BF16.
The released MTP also retains BF16 attention weights; its storage is
counted directly in the measured BPE. The released runtime uses an FP32
convolution cache and FP16+SR SSM cache.

Table 12: Per-module precision across predefined recipe tiers. The
Aggressive column is the released recipe. NVFP4 denotes e2m1-based 4-bit
quantization; FP8 denotes e4m3; W4A16 denotes NVFP4 weights with 16-bit
activations; +SR denotes stochastic rounding. Reference columns denote
precision policies, not complete benchmark serving configurations; the
very-aggressive column was explored but not released. Whole-model BPE
includes quantization scales and BF16-stored router weights: Aggressive
is measured from the released checkpoint; the other columns are
policy-based estimates.

We compute whole-model BPE as $`8S/N`$, where $`S`$ is stored tensor
payload in bytes, including quantization scales, and $`N`$ is the
original parameter count, including vision, MTP, and unquantized
modules. For the released checkpoint, $`S=74.987`$ GB and $`N=124.453`$
billion, giving 4.82 BPE; runtime caches and activations are excluded.

PTQ establishes the initial accuracy of candidate recipes, and QAD is
used to further recover the quantization gap. The released checkpoint
uses the predefined _aggressive_ PTQ recipe followed by SFT-based QAD at
200 iterations. Its median accuracy recovery is 99.62% across 36
benchmarks relative to the BF16-weight reference (also served with FP8
KV cache). For each benchmark, we first average completed runs
separately for the released checkpoint and BF16, compute their score
ratio, and then take the median across benchmarks. Across 36 benchmarks
with token measurements, mean per-benchmark verbosity (Ratio of totals)
increases by 0.44%.

The released checkpoint was evaluated with an FP8 KV cache. Our KV cache
ablations showed comparable accuracy with FP8 and NVFP4. It also
includes the quantized, boosted MTP. As discussed, the checkpoint uses
the predefined _aggressive_ recipe with QAD recovery.

### 4.2 Post-Training Quantization

##### Post-Training Quantization.

We apply post-training quantization (PTQ) using NVIDIA Model Optimizer
to quantize Nemotron 3.5 Super to NVFP4 for efficient inference on
NVIDIA GPUs. The model is a 120B-parameter, 12B-active
mixture-of-experts vision-language model comprising a C-RADIO vision
encoder, a hybrid Mamba/attention language backbone with 512 routed
experts per MoE layer, and a multi-token prediction (MTP) head.

##### Calibration Data.

Activation scales are learned from a calibration set of image–text
pairs, rather than text alone. Images are passed through the vision
encoder so that the language model observes the true input distribution,
including the projected visual tokens. We use the SFT data employed
during post-training as the calibration corpus and calibrate the model
using 1,024 samples.

##### Calibration Parallelism.

We explored several parallelism strategies for efficient calibration:
Pipeline parallelism, Data Parallel (DP) × Expert Parallel (EP), and
FSDP2. Pipeline parallelism left only one GPU active at a time, so we
chose DP $`\times`$ EP. Each of the four ranks processes 256 different
samples, covering all 1,024 calibration samples. The 512 routed experts
are split evenly across the ranks, and the MTP experts are split the
same way. We keep the vision encoder and projector, dense attention
layers, and Mamba layers replicated, since the experts make up most of
the model’s parameters.

##### Weight-Scale Selection.

We compared three weight-scale selection schemes at a fixed NVFP4
quantization tier:

- •
  Max: The scale is determined from the observed absolute maximum.
- •
  MSE: Each block scale is selected to minimize quantization error by
  sweeping over FP8 scale values.
- •
  MSE 4-over-6: MSE-based scale selection restricted to a narrower
  search space.

From the evaluation results (Table
[13](#S4.T13 "Table 13 ‣ Weight-Scale Selection. ‣ 4.2 Post-Training Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
we observe that the MSE algorithm is superior in terms of accuracy
recovery compared to the Max, and MSE 4-over-6. Hence we use the MSE
calibration for the PTQ and AutoQuant recipes.

|                      |       |                   |                      |                      |
| -------------------- | ----- | ----------------- | -------------------- | -------------------- |
| Benchmark            | BF16  | MSE               | Max                  | MSE 4-over-6         |
| MMLU-ProX            | 76.92 | 73.92 ($`-3.00`$) | 72.77 ($`-4.15`$)    | 74.77 ($`-2.15`$)    |
| GPQA-D w/o tools     | 89.52 | 90.03 ($`+0.51`$) | 89.90 ($`+0.38`$)    | 89.52 ($`\pm 0.00`$) |
| APEX Shortlist       | 88.56 | 86.97 ($`-1.60`$) | 87.37 ($`-1.20`$)    | 88.70 ($`+0.13`$)    |
| SciCode Subtask      | 47.52 | 48.37 ($`+0.86`$) | 46.81 ($`-0.70`$)    | 46.79 ($`-0.72`$)    |
| SciCode Problem      | 15.77 | 16.54 ($`+0.77`$) | 15.77 ($`\pm 0.00`$) | 16.35 ($`+0.58`$)    |
| AA-LCR               | 69.00 | 68.06 ($`-0.94`$) | 66.50 ($`-2.50`$)    | 67.00 ($`-2.00`$)    |
| AA-Omni Acc.         | 26.21 | 25.25 ($`-0.96`$) | 25.52 ($`-0.69`$)    | 25.25 ($`-0.96`$)    |
| AA-Omni Non-Halluci. | 70.09 | 71.19 ($`+1.10`$) | 71.98 ($`+1.89`$)    | 71.50 ($`+1.41`$)    |
| HLE w/o tools        | 31.84 | 31.70 ($`-0.14`$) | 30.21 ($`-1.62`$)    | 31.14 ($`-0.70`$)    |
| Tau3 Banking         | 19.46 | 20.10 ($`+0.64`$) | 18.94 ($`-0.52`$)    | 18.94 ($`-0.52`$)    |
| Baby Vision          | 29.81 | 29.47 ($`-0.34`$) | 29.81 ($`\pm 0.00`$) | 28.69 ($`-1.12`$)    |
| V\* w/o CI           | 85.69 | 86.21 ($`+0.52`$) | 86.91 ($`+1.22`$)    | 85.51 ($`-0.17`$)    |
| Score Recovery       | –     | 100.06%           | 98.58%               | 98.15%               |

Table 13: Accuracy for the aggressive tier calibrated using three
different algorithms: MSE, Max, and MSE 4-over-6. Deltas are relative to
the BF16 baseline.

### 4.3 Sensitivity-Driven Mixed Precision

We use ModelOpt AutoQuantize (AutoQuant) to replace hand-authored layer
rules with a sensitivity-constrained mixed-precision search. For each
quantizable module $`i`$ and candidate format
$`q\in\{\mathrm{NVFP4},\mathrm{FP8},\mathrm{BF16}\}`$, AutoQuant
estimates a sensitivity $`S_{i}(q)`$ from the quantization-induced
output error weighted by the squared task-loss gradient. It then solves

|     |     |     |     |
| --- | --- | --- | --- |
|     |

````math
\min_{\{q_{i}\}}\sum_{i}S_{i}(q_{i})\quad\text{s.t.}\quad\sum_{i}n_{i}b(q_{i})\leq\bar{b}\sum_{i}n_{i},
``` |  | (3) |

where $`n_{i}`$ is the number of weights, $`b(q_{i})`$ includes the
modeled storage overhead of format $`q_{i}`$, and $`\bar{b}`$ is the
target effective bitwidth. Operators that must share a deployment
format—such as Q/K/V projections and the routed experts within an MoE
layer—form a single search decision. The vision encoder, cache
quantizers, and fixed MTP recipe are held outside this search.

The sensitivity profile is highly nonuniform: the lm_head accounts for
nearly half of the NVFP4 sensitivity, attention and Mamba projections
are the next most sensitive families, and MoE experts are comparatively
robust. The candidate assignments follow this hierarchy: they
concentrate NVFP4 on the parameter-dominant expert weights, reserve FP8
or BF16 for sensitive attention and Mamba projections, and retain higher
precision for the lm_head.

### 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD)

##### QAD Student (PTQ Initialization)

We initialize each QAD student from a post-training quantized (PTQ)
checkpoint. We derive the quantization configuration from an existing
quantization-reference checkpoint, which comes from ModelOpt AutoQuant
or fixed-recipe PTQ (Sections
[4.2](#S4.SS2 "4.2 Post-Training Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Sensitivity-Driven Mixed Precision ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
The only intentional change is KV cache format; quantization-reference
checkpoints use FP8 KV cache, whereas our PTQ student uses NVFP4 KV
quantization. As such, all results in this subsection refer to
deployment with NVFP4 KV cache. Calibration uses 512 packed multimodal
samples, with variable-length records packed up to 524,288 tokens and
the packing buffer contains 5,000 source samples. The PTQ workflow for
QAD student initialization used eight nodes with four NVIDIA GB200 GPUs
per node (i.e., 32 GPUs total). The complete workflow took approximately
50 minutes per candidate (i.e., about 27 GB200 GPU-hours per PTQ
candidate).

##### Data Blend

We study two data blend settings: the SFT data blend employed during
post-training (100% SFT), and a mixture of 75% SFT data with 25%
teacher-generated RL rollouts. For the RL rollouts we sample 16
trajectories per source record with vLLM. Generation executes the
original RL task workflows and retains environment and tool
interactions. We mask out prompt, conversation-history, tool-output, and
prefilled response-prefix tokens. The rollout mixture provided only
modest benefits in our ablations.

##### QAD Experimental Setup

Each QAD experiment starts from its calibrated PTQ student checkpoint
with a fresh optimizer. We optimize only the weights of language-model
GEMMs with enabled quantizers. All other parameters remain frozen,
including token embeddings, unquantized language-model parameters,
normalization parameters, the vision encoder and projector, and the MTP
module. Router-bias updates are explicitly disabled. The quantization
configuration and calibrated quantizer statistics are restored from the
PTQ checkpoint rather than recalibrated during QAD. We use KL divergence
as our objective. We disable the main hard-target cross-entropy loss,
MTP cross-entropy loss, and MoE load-balancing auxiliary loss and don’t
use intermediate-feature distillation loss. Optimization uses
distributed AdamW with $`\beta_{1}=0.9`$, $`\beta_{2}=0.95`$,
$`\epsilon=10^{-8}`$, weight decay $`0.1`$, and gradient clipping at a
global norm of $`1.0`$. The learning rate increases linearly from zero
to $`5\times 10^{-6}`$ over 9,100 original source records and remains
constant thereafter. We run QAD on 128 nodes with four NVIDIA GB200 GPUs
per node (512 GPUs total). QAD for 100 iterations took about 2 hours
(i.e., 1024 GB200 GPU-hours) for each of our quantization candidates.

##### QAD Results

We evaluated three quantization recipes (for which we generated PTQ and
QAD checkpoints as described above): a) a 4.8 BPE NVFP4 recipe (based on
the quantization configuration selected by the corresponding AutoQuant
run), b) a 4.9 BPE NVFP4 recipe (similarly, based on a 4.8 BPE target
AutoQuant), c) a fixed 4.82 BPE NVFP4 recipe.

Below, we show the results (accuracy and verbosity) for the 4.8 BPE
recipe; specifically, the PTQ checkpoint and the QAD checkpoints for
200, 400, 600 iterations, for both the 100% SFT blend and the 75/25%
SFT/RL blend cases. This initial evaluation sweep was based on an
earlier (i.e., before final) version of the BF16 counterpart. Tables
[14](#S4.T14 "Table 14 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
[15](#S4.T15 "Table 15 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compare the quantized models against that BF16 baseline. PTQ preserves a
median 97.68% of the BF16 accuracy while producing 0.22% fewer
completion tokens on average. QAD consistently closes much of the
remaining accuracy gap, with the evaluated configurations achieving
between 98.36% and 99.18% median score recovery. We obtain the best
overall accuracy–verbosity tradeoff after 200 QAD iterations using 100%
SFT data (99.18% median accuracy recovery - 1.50 percentage points above
PTQ - with only a 1.11% increase in aggregate completion-token count
relative to BF16). Additional QAD iterations were not uniformly
beneficial. For the 100% SFT experiments, the 100 iterations checkpoint
provides the strongest overall accuracy–verbosity tradeoff. The 75/25%
SFT/RL experiments perform best at the intermediate iterations
checkpoint. Overall, our results favor shorter QAD training with 100%
SFT data and show that optimal training duration depends on the selected
data mixture.

We adjusted the QAD experiments based on the above observations for the
final post-trained BF16 checkpoint. In particular, we reduced
checkpointing interval to 100 iterations, used the 100% SFT blend only,
and evaluated QAD checkpoints at 200, 300, and 400 iterations. We
repeated this process for each of the three recipes considered
previously: 4.8, 4.82, and 4.9 BPE. Tables
[16](#S4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
[17](#S4.T17 "Table 17 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
report the accuracy and verbosity results used for final candidate
downselection (wherein selected candidate checkpoints undergo a more
thorough evaluation with a broader set of benchmarks). The effect of
additional QAD iterations is recipe-dependent. For the 4.8-BPE recipe,
moving from 200 to 300 iterations improves both accuracy recovery and
verbosity, reducing the aggregate token increase from 2.30% to 0.75%.
Similarly, for the 4.9-BPE recipe, moving from 200 to 300 iterations
provides substantially better recovery and reduces verbosity change from
1.79% to parity with BF16. The 4.82-BPE recipe behaves differently: its
200-iteration checkpoint provides the strongest accuracy recovery
(almost matches median BF16 accuracy and verbosity), whereas the
300-iteration one is less accurate and slightly less verbose. Overall,
the 4.82-BPE recipe at 200 iterations provides the strongest
accuracy–verbosity tradeoff and constitutes the strongest candidate. We
also selected the best candidate from each of the other two recipes for
a final stage full benchmark evaluation sweep.

Note that although the 400-iteration QAD checkpoints were also
evaluated, they are omitted from Tables
[16](#S4.T16 "Table 16 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
[17](#S4.T17 "Table 17 ‣ QAD Results ‣ 4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
and do not alter the candidate downselection. Our results in fact
indicate that the principal QAD benefits largely saturate by 300
iterations.

### 4.5 KV Cache Quantization Evaluations

As part of our studies, we evaluate both FP8 and NVFP4 quantization
cases; in each case both the key and value caches across attention
layers are quantized in FP8 or NVFP4, respectively. NVFP4 stores 4-bit
E2M1 values with one FP8 E4M3 scale per 16-element block, for an
effective storage cost of 4.5 bits per cached element, excluding
tensor-level scale overhead. Relative to FP8’s 8 bits per element, this
reduces the ideal KV cache payload by $`1-4.5/8=43.75\%`$. This is a
storage calculation, not a measured reduction in runtime memory traffic
or latency; KV cache BPE is separate from the whole-model checkpoint BPE
in Section
[4.1](#S4.SS1 "4.1 Recipe Design ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

##### Evaluation details.

Our reference uses BF16 weights with an FP8 KV cache. The two
configurations in Table
[18](#S4.T18 "Table 18 ‣ Evaluation details. ‣ 4.5 KV Cache Quantization Evaluations ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
use our *aggressive* NVFP4 recipe with QAD at 200 iterations, evaluated
with either (uniform) NVFP4 or FP8 KV cache. For each benchmark, we
average completed runs within each configuration and the reference, then
take the median of the 35 configuration-to-reference score ratios.
Median accuracy recovery is 99.36% for the NVFP4 KV cache configuration
and 99.52% for the released FP8 KV cache configuration (on the same 35
benchmarks).

The NVFP4 KV cache experiments use the original QAD checkpoint, i.e.,
with fixed backbone KV scale values targeting NVFP4 KV cache deployment
(1/6); the FP8 KV cache experiments use the released quantized
checkpoint that contains no KV scales and targets FP8 KV cache
deployment (vLLM defaults to KV scales of 1.0 for FP8 KV cache type).
Both use an FP32 Mamba convolution cache and FP16 SSM cache with
stochastic rounding. The reported median accuracy recovery against the
BF16-weight reference reflects the full weight-and-cache configuration,
not KV quantization alone. The results show broadly comparable aggregate
accuracy in the specific quantization recipe used. The released
quantized NVFP4 checkpoint defaults to FP8 KV cache for
runtime-efficiency and software-readiness considerations despite NVFP4
KV’s storage advantage. Per-layer mixed FP8-key/NVFP4-value (or
vice-versa) configurations are not evaluated in this comparison.

### 4.6 Attention and Projection Precision

To understand the impact of quantization on model behavior, we ablated
three components: global attention’s projection, mamba’s projection, and
the unembedding. We presumed W4A4 routed/shared experts and FP8 KV cache
as the default configuration to test each projection’s sensitivity to
quantization. This reveals a few interesting findings:

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  |  | $`\Delta`$ accuracy (pp) against the BF16 reference |  |  |
| Benchmark | Rollouts | q/k/v BF16 | q/k/v MXFP8 | q/k/v FP8 |
| GraphWalks ($`\leq`$1M) ^(C,S) | $`950\times 1`$ | $`-0.52`$ (t=-0.70) | $`+0.15`$ (t=+0.21) | $`-0.83`$ (t=-1.03) |
| MMLongBench-Doc ^(C,S) | $`1091\times 3`$ | $`-0.76`$ (t=-1.42) | $`-0.90`$ (t=-1.81) | $`-0.34`$ (t=-0.61) |
| AA-LCR ^(S) | $`100\times 16`$ | $`+1.12`$ (t=+0.89) | $`-0.12`$ (t=-0.10) | $`+1.56`$ (t=+1.19) |
| MRCR-128k ^(P) | $`\mathbf{1470\times 3}`$ | $`\mathbf{-2.81}`$ (t=-3.90) | $`\mathbf{-2.65}`$ (t=-3.61) | $`\mathbf{-3.63}`$ (t=-4.97) |

Table 19: Long-context capability: distinction (C, content),
disambiguation (P, position), aggregation (S, summary); all quantized
models serve an FP8 KV cache with W4A4 experts, and differ only in q/k/v
projection precision. $`t=\mathrm{mean}(\Delta)/\mathrm{SE}(\Delta)`$,
where $`\Delta`$ is the per-task difference of task means and the
standard error is taken over *tasks*, not rollouts — so the four rows
are directly comparable.

- •
  The MoE expert weights carry a primary impact on accuracy, causing a
  drift of token distribution in FP4-quantized latent space. This
  becomes critical for runaway loop generation in reasoning failures, if
  a decision boundary of abstention is fragile enough to be affected by
  quantization. No attention projection precision recovers the verbosity
  inflation. Empirically, promoting MoE expert precision and fine-tuning
  on RL rollouts can mitigate this effect (Appendix
  [B.2](#A2.SS2 "B.2 FP4 weight quantization swings the stop-versus-continue margin ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
- •
  Because long-context retrieval is dominated by KV cache precision,
  attention projection has minimal impact on accuracy in most cases.
  However, Table
  [19](#S4.T19 "Table 19 ‣ 4.6 Attention and Projection Precision ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
  shows that *attention score dilution*, sharing a single representable
  level of the quantized attention score among many keys, is more
  pronounced with disambiguation (e.g., MRCR-128k) in 32k+ context
  length. q/k/v projections do not require BF16, but per-tensor FP8’s
  dynamic range constrains attention logit granularity. MXFP8’s
  per-block scaling helps mitigate the quality degradation. Attention’s
  output projection is structurally exposed but tolerant to FP8
  quantization (Appendix
  [B.3](#A2.SS3 "B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
- •
  Mamba output projection also has a minimal impact on accuracy, but
  quantizing its weights to FP4 inflates generation length. Its input
  activations are separately exposed: the SSM gate emits near-zero yet
  never exactly zero values, which flush to zero under FP4 activation
  quantization. Rotation or higher precision can mitigate this effect in
  activation, but does not address the verbosity inflation without
  weight realignment (Appendix
  [B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Mamba output projection: FP4 weights, and the sensitivity is on generation length ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
- •
  The lm_head weights of unembedding do not change accuracy even with
  FP4 quantization. However, FP4 amplifies the perturbation of the
  stop-versus-continue margin. To maintain token generation verbosity,
  the lm_head should stay at FP8 weights or above (Appendix
  [B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 The unembedding: FP8 weights, and the sensitivity is on termination ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).

### 4.7 MTP Quantization

To reduce drafting latency, we quantize the MTP module of Nemotron 3.5
Super NVFP4 checkpoint. This applies only to Nemotron 3.5 Super; the MTP
module of Nemotron 3.5 Lightning is not quantized. The MTP module
contains an attention layer and an MoE layer. It does not carry its own
output projection: the released checkpoint stores a single lm_head,
shared between target verification and MTP drafting. Profiling shows
that the MoE experts and the lm_head account for most of the drafting
latency. We therefore quantize the MTP experts using NVFP4 W4A4. The MTP
attention weights remain BF16, with only the K/V BMM quantizers at FP8.

Isolating the effect of MTP quantization requires holding the backbone
fixed. Against the NVFP4 backbone with a BF16 MTP ($`AL=4.612`$ at draft
length 7), quantizing the MTP experts reduces AL to $`AL=4.592`$ (Table
[20](#S4.T20 "Table 20 ‣ Construction. ‣ 4.7 MTP Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).

To assess the possible speedup from MTP and lm_head quantization, we
simulated drafting and verification latencies at draft length $`5`$, on
a single GB200 GPU, with a quantized backbone. Our simulated latency
projections show that when the MTP and lm_head are both unquantized,
drafting amounts for $`23.5\%`$ of the total latency, and the drafting
latency splits to $`49.6\%`$ MTP latency and $`51.4\%`$ lm_head latency.
Therefore, using quantization to reduce the latency of both components
is beneficial to reduce the total drafting time, if the drop in
acceptance length is not too big.

According to our projections, quantizing the MTP layer is expected to
reduce $`47\%`$ of its latency, and quantizing the lm_head to FP8 and
W4A16 precisions is expected to reduce its latency by $`49\%`$ and
$`71\%`$, respectively. Quantizing the target model’s lm_head was
initially considered higher risk because it directly affects the target
logits and, consequently, model quality. As a fallback, we experimented
with various combinations using separate target and MTP lm_head
precisions, and implemented support in *vLLM 0.30.0* for serving the
model in this mixed configuration. This allows the target lm_head to
remain in BF16 while using a lower-precision lm_head for drafting. The
AL results and expected speedups over the using MTP and lm_head are
described in Table
[20](#S4.T20 "Table 20 ‣ Construction. ‣ 4.7 MTP Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

FP8 quantization of the target lm_head was ultimately selected, thus a
single FP8 lm_head serves both target verification and MTP drafting, and
no separate output head is required. The separate-precision support
remains available for models where quantizing the target lm_head
degrades quality.

##### KV-cache coupling.

The MTP cannot use a different KV-cache precision from the backbone. MTP
KV precision therefore follows the backbone. We empirically validated
this does not degrade ALs, making the coupling a structural constraint
rather than a quality trade-off.

##### Construction.

The released checkpoints were produced by quantizing the boosted MTP
(Section
[3.7](#S3.SS7 "3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
separately and splicing it into the selected quantized backbone, rather
than co-quantizing both in a single pass. The splice transfers the MTP
weights and their scales while leaving the surrounding configuration
unchanged, so source and destination must agree exactly on the MTP
tensor set — names, shapes and dtypes. Co-quantizing the MTP together
with the backbone removes this coupling and is the preferred direction
for future releases.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Target model |  | MTP drafter |  | Acceptance length |  |  Projected speedup |
| Backbone | LM head | Experts | LM head | DL=5 | DL=7 | DL=5 |
| BF16 | BF16 | BF16 | BF16 | $`4.115`$ | $`4.612`$ | - |
| NVFP4 | BF16 | BF16 | BF16 | $`4.109`$ | $`4.592`$ | 1 |
| NVFP4 | BF16 | NVFP4 | BF16 | $`4.066`$ | $`4.518`$ | 1.043 |
| NVFP4 | BF16 | NVFP4 | FP8 | $`4.069`$ | $`4.514`$ | 1.114 |
| NVFP4 | BF16 | NVFP4 | W4A16 | $`4.031`$ | $`4.469`$ | 1.136 |
| NVFP4 | FP8 | NVFP4 | FP8 | $`4.063`$ | $`4.520`$ | 1.127 |
| NVFP4 | FP8 | NVFP4 | W4A16 | $`4.025`$ | $`4.471`$ | 1.150 |

MTP-drafter precisions, and projected speedups over the baseline.
Results are measured on the SPEED-Bench qualitative split at

### 4.8 Parallel Drafter Quantization

Following a similar procedure to MTP quantization, we applied
weight-only PTQ to improve the drafting efficiency of a DSpark ([Cheng
et al., 2026](#bib.bib12)) module for Nemotron Lightning 3.5 and
LiLiCorr ([Rusanovsky et al., 2026](#bib.bib70)) module for Nemotron 3.5
Super (see Section
[5.7](#S5.SS7 "5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
Our experiments showed that in memory-bound scenarios, e.g. on edge
devices with low concurrency, parallel drafters benefit greatly from
weight quantization, as this reduces the memory overhead from loading
drafter parameters. For Nemotron 3.5 Super, we observed that quantizing
LiLiCorr’s linear layers to NVFP4 while keeping the activations at BF16,
resulted in a 11% throughput improvement on DGX Spark without
meaningfully sacrificing acceptance lengths, as shown in Table
[28](#S5.T28 "Table 28 ‣ Evaluation ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
Further quantizing the LM head improved throughput by another 5%. We
identified model-specific quantization settings that performed well both
in terms of drafter accuracy and overall throughput on DGX Spark.
Additional details are in Table
[21](#S4.T21 "Table 21 ‣ 4.8 Parallel Drafter Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Layer group | Quantization-config name | Layer type |
| General backbone | mlp.\* | Gate/up/down projections |
|  | fc | Fully connected projection |
|  | lm_head | Language-model output head |
| DSpark-specific | markov_head.markov_w2 | Markov output projection |
| LiLiCorr-specific | lilicorr.context_proj | Context/hidden-state/token projections |
|  | lilicorr.pass_hidden_proj |  |
|  | lilicorr.token_proj |  |
|  | lilicorr.attn_out_proj | Attention output projection |
|  | lilicorr.mlp | LiLiCorr MLP projections |

### 4.9 Lightning 3.5 Evaluation and Results

We evaluate Nemotron 3.5 Lightning using the NeMo Gym evaluation
framework described for Super 3.5. Lightning-specific settings are
available in the recipes folder and reproducibility tutorial¹⁸¹⁸ 18
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/lightning-3.5/reproducibility.md](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/lightning-3.5/reproducibility.md).
For evaluation, we serve both the BF16 and NVFP4 checkpoints with FP8 KV
cache and FP32 Mamba SSM cache. SSM cache is kept in FP32 as it was not
a limiter on DGX Spark for low batch (\< 4) use cases. In the W4A16
NVFP4 checkpoint, the routed experts, shared experts, and language-model
output head use 4-bit NVFP4 weights with 16-bit activations. The Mamba
input and output projections use FP8 weights and activations, while
attention projections remain in BF16. We select the W4A16 NVFP4
configuration using the same PTQ/QAD procedure, and compare it with BF16
under matched evaluation and serving settings. Table
[22](#S4.T22 "Table 22 ‣ 4.9 Lightning 3.5 Evaluation and Results ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the benchmark scores¹⁹¹⁹ 19 Evaluation results are subject to
run-to-run variation due to stochastic sampling and, where applicable,
judge variability. Scores may therefore differ from those reported on
the model cards. between BF16 and W4A16 NVFP4, reporting a Median
Accuracy Recovery of 99.24% and a Ratio of Totals Verbosity change of
$`-3.63\%`$ across benchmarks.

### 4.10 Super 3.5 VL Evaluation and Results

We evaluate Nemotron 3.5 Super on a comprehensive suite of benchmarks
spanning agentic capabilities, reasoning and knowledge, multimodal,
conversational ability and instruction following, long-context
understanding, and multilingual performance. All evaluation results for
Nemotron 3.5 Super and baselines were collected via Nemo Gym²⁰²⁰ 20
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym).
For reproducibility purposes, more details on the evaluation settings
can be found in the Nemo Gym recipes folder and the corresponding
reproducibility tutorial²¹²¹ 21
[https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/super-3.5/reproducibility.md](https://github.com/NVIDIA-NeMo/Gym/blob/main/nemotron_recipes/super-3.5/reproducibility.md).

We evaluate the candidate NVFP4 checkpoints generated from PTQ and QAD
to select the most accuracy preserving and verbosity maintaining
candidate compared to the baseline BF16. The evaluation and serving
parameters are common between the BF16 and NVFP4 for fair comparison.

We measure model intelligence with two metrics: Median Accuracy Recovery
and Ratio of Totals Verbosity. The former is computed by averaging
accuracy over completed runs for the NVFP4 and BF16 checkpoints, divide
the NVFP4 average by the BF16 average, and report the median of these
ratios across benchmarks. The latter is computed by averaging output
tokens per trajectory over all trajectories and runs. We then sum these
averages across benchmarks for each checkpoint and report the percent
change of the NVFP4 total relative to the BF16 total.

Terminal-Bench shows a +0.2% change in mean output tokens per trajectory
over *agent-completed rollouts* and +0.8% in the median over all
rollouts, but +15.7% in the mean over *all rollouts*, because roughly
4.5% of non-completing runaway trajectories (about 700k tokens each)
dominate the mean. To avoid skewing the verbosity metrics, we exclude
the non-completed rollouts for Terminal-Bench; if included the Verbosity
Change (Ratio of Totals) increases to $`2.99\%`$.

We run each benchmark twice and average their scores to account for
run-to-run variability, in addition to the number of repeats specified
by the benchmark.

summarizes evals between BF16 and NVFP4 checkpoints using FP8 KV cache.

and recovery are calculated from accuracy.

## 5 Inference

Nemotron 3.5 Super was designed for interactive, long-context agentic
workloads, where requests commonly share system instructions, tool
definitions, or retrieved context and then extend that context over
multiple turns. Effective serving performance therefore depends not only
on peak throughput or single-request latency, but also on preserving
prefix reuse while sustaining an interactive per-user generation rate.

For system tuning, we use a 20-turn proxy workload with 2,048 input
tokens and 1,024 output tokens per turn; the shared prompt and prior
conversation are eligible for prefix reuse on subsequent turns. We call
a serving point *usable* when its reported per-user generation rate is
at least 50 tokens/s. Among these points, we seek the highest aggregate
output throughput. The selected operating point depends on the platform
and serving configuration.

This regime places particular emphasis on three parts of the serving
stack. First, prefix reuse must remain effective for a hybrid
Mamba-Attention model when speculative decoding changes the position
from which execution resumes. Second, kernel selection must be
controllable at finer granularity than a single global policy because
the best kernels at a balanced operating point need not be those that
win at either endpoint of the latency-throughput curve. Third, the same
model must map efficiently to accelerators with different memory
capacities, bandwidths, and quantization support. We address these
requirements in vLLM and characterize their effect below.

### 5.1 Efficiency

Figure
[10](#S5.F10 "Figure 10 ‣ 5.1 Efficiency ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the max-throughput at 50 tps/user, and min-latency performance
of Nemotron 3.5 Super on H100 and GB200 for 50k prefix and 20 turns of
2k/1k.

Figure 10: Serving performance using vLLM on the 20-turn agentic proxy
workload, with 2K input tokens and 1K output tokens per turn and
ReplaySSM disabled. The graphs compare NVFP4 and BF16 maximum throughput
at \>=50 tokens/s/user and minimum latency.

### 5.2 Performance across deployment platforms

##### Cross-platform performance.

We validated Nemotron 3.5 Super across NVIDIA datacenter and local
systems, including A100, H100, B200, GB200 and GB300 platforms, DGX
Spark and DGX Station, and RTX-class GPUs. These platforms span multiple
architecture generations and deployment scales. Rather than impose one
serving configuration everywhere, we tune quantization and speculative
decoding for each platform. The objective remains the same: maximize
aggregate throughput over the 20-turn proxy workload while sustaining at
least 50 output tokens/s/user.

Figure
[11](#S5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Cross-platform performance. ‣ 5.2 Performance across deployment platforms ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the resulting frontier with a Qwen model of comparable size on
a representative platform. Nemotron 3.5 Super provides higher
performance in the region relevant to interactive serving. The
comparison is made at a matched workload, with checkpoint, precision,
and system configuration reported in the final caption; points below 50
output tokens/s/user are excluded from the usable region even when they
offer high aggregate throughput.

Figure 11: Serving performance using vllm for the 20-turn agentic proxy
workload, with 2K input tokens and 1K output tokens per turn. The graph
shows the max-throughput of each model at tps/user \>= 50 .

##### Performance Modeling and Analysis

Our internal performance model attributes the advantage in Figure
[11](#S5.F11 "Figure 11 ‣ Cross-platform performance. ‣ 5.2 Performance across deployment platforms ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
to how Nemotron 3.5 Super handles per-request state, how many tokens
each decoding step yields, and how much data its experts exchange. In
each decode step the weights are read once and shared by the whole
batch, while every request reads and updates its own KV cache and
recurrent state, which must also stay in GPU memory. Throughput
therefore depends on how cheaply each request produces a token and on
how many requests share the cost of reading the weights.

Like the Qwen models, Nemotron 3.5 Super is a hybrid, but it has fewer
attention layers, with narrower key-value heads, and leaves the rest of
sequence mixing to Mamba-2 layers whose state does not grow with
context, so its per-request memory grows much more slowly as agentic
sessions accumulate history. At these lengths each Qwen3.8 request needs
more GPU memory, so fewer requests fit on a GPU, which limits how many
share each weight read; its sparse attention reduces how much KV cache
is read per step, but not how much must be stored. Qwen3.5 stores about
as much KV cache as Qwen3.8 and reads all of it on every step, so each
of its requests costs more in both memory and bandwidth than a Nemotron
3.5 Super request.

The MTP head reaches a higher average acceptance length than both Qwen
models under probabilistic draft sampling (Table
[9](#S3.T9 "Table 9 ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super Results. ‣ 3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
so each step yields more tokens per request, and LatentMoE exchanges
latent vectors that are smaller than the Qwen models’ hidden states,
which reduces the expert-parallel communication per token. A large
per-request cost in Nemotron 3.5 Super’s decoding is updating the Mamba
state, which speculative decoding writes out for every token verified in
a step because the number of accepted tokens is not known in advance;
ReplaySSM (Section
[5.4](#S5.SS4 "5.4 Kernel control at the target operating point ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
reduces this traffic and the memory it requires.

### 5.3 Prefix reuse and speculative decoding

##### Hybrid-model cache checkpoints are coarse.

For a pure Attention model, prefix reuse is represented by per-token KV
state stored in relatively small cache pages. A hybrid model must
additionally recover the recurrent Mamba state at the position where
computation resumes. This state contains the complete sequence summary
for a layer and is much larger per cached position than an Attention KV
entry. vLLM therefore matches the cache-page sizes of the Attention and
Mamba cache groups by byte size. On our target Mamba-2 model, one Mamba
cache page occupies at least as many bytes as approximately 4K tokens of
Attention KV cache. Consequently, Mamba states are normally retained on
a much coarser token grid than Attention KV state.

This granularity matters when prefix caching is combined with
speculative decoding. Suppose the target model obtains a prefix-cache
hit through token $`T`$. To draft token $`T+1`$, the drafter needs the
target model’s intermediate hidden state for token $`T`$. The prefix
cache contains the Attention KV state and a checkpointed Mamba state,
but not that intermediate hidden state. vLLM reconstructs it by resuming
the target model from the most recent stored Mamba checkpoint. If the
Mamba block spans $`L_{\mathrm{page}}`$ tokens, the nearest resumable
state may be at $`T-L_{\mathrm{page}}`$, forcing the target model to
replay tokens $`T-L_{\mathrm{page}}`$ through $`T`$ solely to recover
the hidden state at token $`T`$.

We improved this path by optionally retaining an additional Mamba cache
page near the speculative-decoding replay boundary. For example, if a
workload uses a 16-token fine-grained prefix-match unit, the additional
checkpoint can be placed near $`T-16`$ rather than
$`T-L_{\mathrm{page}}`$. A subsequent request then replays only the
final 16 target-model tokens instead of an entire Mamba block. The
scheduler and cache manager jointly select and publish a valid
checkpoint at the shared prefix boundary, avoiding stale recurrent state
while trading one additional Mamba page for substantially less
target-model recomputation.²²²² 22
[https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53945](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/53945)

The issue is less pronounced for non-SSM models because their cache
pages span far fewer tokens, so replaying from the preceding page
boundary is inexpensive. It is also more important for Nemotron 3.5
Super than for hybrid SSM models with smaller recurrent states:
Mamba-2’s larger cache-page footprint makes $`L_{\mathrm{page}}`$ larger
and amplifies the work avoided by the finer checkpoint. The resulting
mechanism makes prefix caching and speculative decoding composable:
prefix caching removes repeated prefill work, while speculative decoding
reduces the number of target-model decode steps after prefill.

### 5.4 Kernel control at the target operating point

##### Per-quantization GEMM backend selection.

The kernel that maximizes throughput at saturation is not necessarily
the kernel that gives the best end-to-end result at an intermediate
batch size. Our target lies in precisely this intermediate regime: we
need enough batching to obtain high aggregate throughput while
preserving at least 50 output tokens/s/user. Furthermore, a
mixed-precision checkpoint may contain linear layers with different
quantization schemes, whose GEMM shapes and best backends differ. A
single process-wide backend selection therefore couples decisions that
should be tuned independently.

The global linear backend remains the default, while a kernel
configuration can override it for a specific quantization scheme - for
example, selecting a specialized backend for NVFP4 W4A16 layers while
leaving other linear layers on the platform default. This finer control
lets a deployment choose the best-supported GEMM implementation for each
quantization scheme and accelerator without maintaining separate model
artifacts or forcing every quantized layer through the same path. It
also makes the serving configuration portable: Hopper, Blackwell, and
compact systems can use different backend maps while preserving the same
model and workload-level objective. ²³²³ 23
[https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51204](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/51204)

##### Checkpointing replay for SSM cache quantization.

One inference performance improvement grows out of a suggestion in the
Nemotron v3 Ultra Technical report. During a decoding step of the
Mamba-2 layer, for non-speculative decoding we have the recurrent state
$`S`$ and per-token inputs key vector $`k`$, value vector $`v`$, decay
scalar $`\alpha`$, and query vector $`q`$. The new recurrent state is
then $`S_{new}=\alpha S_{old}+k^{\top}v`$, and the output
$`o=qS_{new}`$. For speculative decoding we do this for the $`T`$
incoming tokens’ worth of input, writing out each possible new state as
we do not know how many tokens will be accepted. This results in
decoding being extremely memory bound, and it also limits batch_size \*
draft_length for speculative decoding due to limited memory capacity.

We have since implemented a "checkpointing replay" method. We store a
checkpointed state $`S_{ckpt}`$ and store the $`k_{i},v_{i},\alpha_{i}`$
seen since the checkpoint in a small buffer. As
$`S=(\prod\alpha_{i})S_{ckpt}+\sum_{i}(\prod_{j>i+1}\alpha_{j})k_{i}^{\top}v_{i}`$
we can reconstruct the state on-demand, and with no quantization error
beyond what’s in $`S_{ckpt}`$. Rejected tokens are handled by simply
decrementing a counter, and new inputs are appended to the buffer. To
generate output tokens, we can either reconstruct the state or we can
generate output tokens more cheaply directly, by noting that (ignoring
the $`\alpha`$ terms for exposition)
$`qS=qS_{ckpt}+\sum_{i}(qk_{i}^{\top})v_{i}`$. When our buffer fills up,
we ’flush’ or ’fold’ it by reconstructing the state and writing it out.
For speculative decoding we only include the accepted tokens from our
history, with that step’s tokens seeding the start of the new history
buffer.

This amortizes the state write over our buffer length, reducing our
per-step state-related memory traffic from $`T+1`$ to roughly $`1+1/L`$.
It also lets us amortize any computational overhead associated with
writing the state, such as randomness generation, stochastic rounding,
or scaling calculations. Especially for speculative decoding, the
resulting formulae look a lot like chunked prefill, with the parts moved
around. Like chunked prefill, the FLOP cost goes up but we get to move
CUDA core work, namely matvecs and outer products, over to Tensor Cores,
so actual compute time goes down. In summary, this method:

- •
  Reduces memory traffic on an IO bound operation.
- •
  Reduces compute time by shifting work to Tensor Cores.
- •
  Reduces quantization error, by quantizing less often.
- •
  Enables larger batch sizes and speculation lengths.

For more details, see https://github.com/NVIDIA/TensorRT-LLM/pull/14203.
We note this technique was discovered concurrently and independently in
the Dao lab, where it is called ReplaySSM, as described in [Liou et al.
(2026)](#bib.bib47) and in their blog post
(https://dao-lab.ai/blog/2026/replayssm/); we subsequently collaborated
with them on the paper and on bringing it to vLLM.

Table
[24](#S5.T24 "Table 24 ‣ Checkpointing replay for SSM cache quantization. ‣ 5.4 Kernel control at the target operating point ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares ReplaySSM enabled and disabled for Nemotron Super 3.5 on a
single NVIDIA GB200 GPU, using a 16-step ReplaySSM buffer and default
KV-cache dtype. Across the selected matched configurations, ReplaySSM
improves output throughput by 4.7–6.4% and per-user generation
throughput by 6.4–12.5%.

The workload comprises 20 turns with a 51,200-token shared prefix,
2,048-token input per turn, and 1,024-token output.

|-------|-----|----------------|-------|-------|---------------|------|--------|
|       |     | Output TPS/GPU |       |       | Gen. TPS/user |      |        |

| 64    | 7   | 4,629          | 4,846 | +4.7% | 81.3          | 91.5 | +12.5% |

vLLM, using a 16-step buffer. MTP denotes speculative tokens; TPS
denotes tokens/s. Gains are relative to OFF, calculated before rounding.
````
