````math
### 5.5 Reducing the cost of Mamba checkpoints

##### Internal prefill checkpoints.

In aligned Mamba cache mode, a reusable recurrent state must be
materialized at a cache-block boundary. The scheduling path can enforce
this by ending a prefill step at that boundary and issuing an additional
model forward for the remainder of the prompt. Although this produces
the required state, the split repeats fixed forward-pass overhead and
adds another traversal of the Attention, MoE, routing, and
tensor-parallel communication paths.

Internal prefill checkpointing was initially implemented in vLLM for
Kimi K3. Building on that work, we adapted the shared checkpoint builder
and exporter to Mamba-2 in PR \#57329.²⁴²⁴ 24
[https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57329](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57329)
The Mamba scan already materializes intermediate recurrent states at
logical chunk boundaries. The adapted path arranges for a logical chunk
to end at the desired cache boundary, extracts the state and convolution
window there, and writes that checkpoint into the paged cache while the
full prompt remains in one model forward. A prompt that previously
required an additional forward after the checkpoint can therefore be
processed in a single forward without sacrificing the reusable Mamba
state.

Taken together, these changes target the regime that matters most for
Nemotron 3.5 Super deployment: shared and growing contexts, interactive
decode rates, and hardware-specific configurations chosen from the
useful portion of the throughput-latency frontier. Prefix-aware
speculative decoding reduces both repeated prefill and decode work,
per-quantization backend selection exposes the kernel choices needed to
tune balanced operating points, and internal Mamba checkpoints reduce
the cost of creating the reusable state on which the workload depends.

### 5.6 Disaggregated serving on NVIDIA Vera Rubin

We serve the Nemotron 3.5 Super VL NVFP4 checkpoint with NVIDIA Dynamo
and vLLM on NVIDIA Vera Rubin (VR200) GPUs, with one GPU per model
instance (no tensor parallelism). The workload replays multi-turn
agentic coding sessions from SWE-bench Pro traces, with long contexts
and high prefix reuse across turns. Speculative decoding uses the MTP
head with a fixed acceptance length of 4.06 and draft length 5. A usable
point keeps the median output speed at or above 50 tokens/s/user and the
median time to first token (TTFT) below 5 s. We report output tokens/s
per GPU at the highest-throughput load that meets this target.

Prefill reads the prompt and builds the KV cache, the stored attention
state of earlier tokens. Decode then generates the output tokens. We
compare two deployments on 11 GPUs each. The aggregated deployment runs
11 independent instances that each do prefill and decode. A
KV-cache-aware router sends each request to an instance. The
disaggregated deployment uses 7 GPUs for prefill only and 4 GPUs for
decode only. After prefill, the KV cache moves from the prefill GPU to
the decode GPU.

Disaggregated serving gives 10.3% more output tokens/s per GPU than
aggregated serving (Table
[25](#S5.T25 "Table 25 ‣ 5.6 Disaggregated serving on NVIDIA Vera Rubin ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
At its best point, the median output speed per user is also 61% higher.
The median TTFT increases from 0.49 s to 1.12 s, but stays well below
the 5 s target. Prefill and decode no longer share a GPU. As a result,
the decode GPUs run larger decode batches without long prefills between
decode steps.

|               |       |         |          |          |
|---------------|-------|---------|----------|----------|
| Deployment    | Conc. | TPS/GPU | TPS/user | TTFT (s) |
| Aggregated    | 616   | 2,969   | 96.6     | 0.49     |
| Disaggregated | 528   | 3,276   | 155.9    | 1.12     |
| Gain          |       | +10.3%  | +61%     |          |

Table 25: Best usable point for Nemotron 3.5 Super VL NVFP4 on 11 NVIDIA
VR200 GPUs with Dynamo and vLLM. The workload replays SWE-bench Pro
agentic coding sessions. A usable point has a median output speed of at
least 50 tokens/s/user and a median TTFT below 5 s. Disaggregated uses 7
prefill and 4 decode GPUs. TPS/user and TTFT are medians. MTP uses a
fixed acceptance length of 4.06 and draft length 5. TPS denotes
tokens/s.

##### Precision and kernels.

Both deployments use the engine settings that follow. The weights use
NVFP4 and the KV cache uses FP8. The Mamba state cache uses FP16 with
stochastic rounding, as in Section 4. The compiler fuses each attention
operation with the quantization of its output. The engine captures CUDA
graphs for full and piecewise model steps. In decode attention, one wave
of GPU work can allow only one or two splits of the KV cache per
request. The multi-wave KV split then splits the KV cache further and
runs the work in several waves. We added this option to FlashInfer in PR
\#6331²⁵²⁵ 25
[https://github.com/flashinfer-ai/flashinfer/pull/6331](https://github.com/flashinfer-ai/flashinfer/pull/6331)
and to vLLM in PR \#61077.²⁶²⁶ 26
[https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61077](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/61077)

##### Speculative decoding and cache reuse.

The MTP head drafts 5 tokens per step. Prefix caching stores Mamba
states at KV-cache block boundaries, plus extra checkpoints at prefixes
that several requests share. A vLLM option (disable_eagle_block_drop)
lets a cache hit keep its last full block when speculative decoding is
on. Each aggregated instance and each prefill GPU also has a 240 GiB KV
cache tier in host (CPU) memory. This tier keeps the prefixes of earlier
turns after the GPU cache evicts them.

##### Routing.

Each worker publishes KV-cache events, so the router knows which cache
blocks each worker holds. Session affinity sends all turns of one
session to the same worker, so later turns find their prefix in its
cache. In the disaggregated deployment, NIXL moves the KV cache from the
prefill GPU to the decode GPU.

### 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters

Alongside the native MTP head (Sections
[2.1](#S2.SS1 "2.1 Multi-Token Prediction (MTP) ‣ 2 Pretraining ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
and
[3.7](#S3.SS7 "3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
we release LiLiCorr ([Rusanovsky et al., 2026](#bib.bib70)), a
standalone drafter for Nemotron 3.5 Super that targets low-concurrency
serving, where decoding is strongly memory-bandwidth bound. This release
builds on our earlier parallel-drafter work for Nemotron 3.5 Lightning,
for which we trained and released separate DFlash ([Chen et al.,
2026a](#bib.bib9)) and DSpark ([Cheng et al., 2026](#bib.bib12))
checkpoints. The Nemotron 3.5 Lightning DFlash uses non-causal,
full-sequence grouped-query attention, whereas the released DSpark uses
causal grouped-query attention with sliding-window attention in every
layer and per-head attention sinks. Bringing these models to release
also required W4A16 post-training quantization (Section
[4.8](#S4.SS8 "4.8 Parallel Drafter Quantization ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
and vLLM optimizations for the DSpark Markov head.

In the low-concurrency regime targeted here, verifying a block of draft
tokens costs similarly to generating a single token, so the speedup is
determined jointly by the acceptance length and the latency of producing
the draft. The recursive MTP head drafts one token per pass, so its
drafting latency grows with the draft length, while parallel drafters
propose all draft tokens in a single forward pass whose latency is
nearly independent of the draft length. MTP is part of the model
checkpoint and requires no additional weights, whereas DFlash, DSpark,
and LiLiCorr are separate drafter checkpoints aimed at this regime.

A parallel drafter is trained on the marginal distribution at each block
position, henceforth a slot, so tokens selected independently at each
slot can be individually plausible yet jointly incoherent. DSpark
addresses this with a semi-autoregressive design. It keeps the drafter
body parallel and adds a lightweight sequential head that biases the
logits at each slot using a low-rank transition from the token drafted
at the previous slot. In the full DSpark design, a separate confidence
head estimates the probability that each draft prefix will be accepted,
allowing the serving engine to adapt the verification length to the
request and system load. The Lightning 3.5 release used the lightweight
Markov correction but omitted this confidence head. LiLiCorr instead
uses a lightweight, single-pass correlation head to score compatibility
across slots.

#### 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5

##### Introduction.

At each of the $`B-1`$ drafted slots, LiLiCorr retains the $`K`$ most
probable tokens of the drafter body’s marginal as candidates and
processes the resulting $`(B-1)\times K`$ candidate lattice jointly with
a small Transformer, so that each candidate is represented in the
context of all others in the block. For the $`k`$-th candidate at slot
$`s`$, with $`k=1,\dots,K`$ and $`s=1,\dots,B-1`$, the head emits a
unit-norm *out* vector $`\mathbf{o}_{s,k}`$ and *in* vector
$`\mathbf{i}_{s,k}`$. Slot $`0`$ holds the last token committed by the
target. Since this token is already fixed, it is the only candidate at
slot $`0`$ and carries a single out vector $`\mathbf{o}_{0,1}`$. The
compatibility of candidate $`k`$ at slot $`s`$ with candidate
$`k^{\prime}`$ at slot $`s+1`$ is the cosine similarity between the out
vector of the former and the in vector of the latter:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\phi(s,k,k^{\prime})=\sigma\cos\!\left(\mathbf{o}_{s,k},\,\mathbf{i}_{s+1,k^{\prime}}\right),\qquad s=0,\dots,B-2,
``` |  | (4) |

where $`\sigma`$ is a fixed scale. Writing $`\hat{c}_{s}`$ for the index
of the candidate committed at slot $`s`$, with $`\hat{c}_{0}=1`$, the
block is committed from left to right, each slot selecting the candidate
most compatible with its committed predecessor:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\hat{c}_{s}=\arg\max_{k}\,\phi\!\left(s-1,\,\hat{c}_{s-1},\,k\right),\qquad s=1,\dots,B-1.
``` |  | (5) |

Under probabilistic draft sampling, each slot is instead drawn from the
head’s distribution over its candidates at the request’s temperature.
All vectors come from one pass of the head and the pairwise scores in
Eq.
([4](#S5.E4 "In Introduction. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"))
reduce to batched matrix products, so only the walk is sequential, and
it requires no further network evaluation. Verification is unchanged, so
the output distribution of the target is preserved exactly.

##### Setup.

We train the drafter body and the LiLiCorr head jointly in NVIDIA Model
Optimizer (ModelOpt) ([NVIDIA Corporation, 2024–2026](#bib.bib61)), in a
streaming configuration suited to a target of this size. Replicas of the
target’s language model are served in BF16 by vLLM ([Kwon et al.,
2023](#bib.bib42)), each with tensor parallelism of four, and stream the
hidden states of five captured target layers, together with the final
hidden state, to $`64`$ trainer GPUs. The target’s next-token
distribution, which the objective below requires, is reconstructed on
the trainer from the final hidden state through the target’s own LM
head, so the target body is never replicated on the trainer ranks. The
drafter uses a block of $`B=8`$ positions, i.e., $`7`$ drafted slots.
Each of the five layers of the drafter body wraps the attention and MLP
sublayers in a grouped dynamic convolution inspired by DFlash2 ([Inco
AI, 2026](#bib.bib34)), which lets neighboring slots exchange local
information while the block remains parallel. The LiLiCorr head keeps
the configuration of the original work. The resulting drafter has
$`1.13`$B parameters, of which $`37`$M belong to the head.

##### Objective.

The original work trains the drafter body with a cross-entropy against
the tokens of the target-generated training responses, weighted by the
exponential position decay of DFlash. We depart from this recipe and, as
in MTP boosting (Section
[3.7](#S3.SS7 "3.7 MTP Boosting ‣ 3 Post-Training ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")),
instead distill the full next-token distribution $`p_{M}`$ of the target
model $`M`$, which is available from the streamed hidden states, while
keeping the same decay. Consider a training block that starts at
position $`t`$ of a training sequence $`x`$, with $`x_{t}`$ at slot
$`0`$, so that slot $`s`$ predicts the token $`x_{t+s}`$ from the prefix
$`x_{<t+s}`$. Its loss is

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DFlash}}=-\sum_{s=1}^{B-1}w_{s}\sum_{v\in\mathcal{V}}p_{M}\!\left(v\mid x_{<t+s}\right)\log p_{\theta,s}(v),\qquad w_{s}=\exp\!\left(-\frac{s-1}{\beta}\right),
``` |  | (6) |

where $`\mathcal{V}`$ is the vocabulary, $`p_{\theta,s}`$ is the
marginal distribution over $`\mathcal{V}`$ that the drafter body, with
parameters $`\theta`$, predicts at slot $`s`$, and $`\beta`$ sets the
decay. The loss is averaged over blocks whose starting positions are
sampled from each sequence. Up to a constant, this is the forward KL
divergence from $`p_{M}`$ to $`p_{\theta,s}`$. Because the training
responses are sampled at temperature $`1`$, a response token need not be
the target’s most likely one. The full distribution supervises both the
target’s most likely token, which greedy verification tests, and the
shape of its distribution, which sampled verification tests.

Let $`c_{s}\in\{1,\dots,K\}`$ denote the index of the candidate chosen
at slot $`s`$, with $`c_{0}=1`$. The head defines, at each slot, a
distribution over its $`K`$ candidates conditioned on the candidate
chosen at the previous slot,

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
P_{\theta,\psi}\!\left(c_{s}=k\mid c_{s-1}\right)=\mathrm{softmax}_{k}\,\phi\!\left(s-1,\,c_{s-1},\,k\right),
``` |  | (7) |

where $`\psi`$ denotes the parameters of the head. The distribution
depends on $`\theta`$ as well, because the head’s inputs include the
log-probabilities of the candidates under $`p_{\theta,s}`$ and the
drafter body’s hidden states. The head’s loss terms therefore train the
drafter body as well. Let $`g_{s}`$ be the index of the candidate equal
to the ground-truth token $`x_{t+s}`$, when it is among the $`K`$
candidates, with $`g_{0}=1`$. The oracle prefix of a block is its
longest leading run of slots $`1,\dots,m`$ that all contain the
ground-truth token, and $`\mathcal{S}`$ collects the slots on the oracle
prefixes of all blocks in a training batch. The head is teacher-forced
on the ground-truth predecessor, and its cross-entropy term is that of
the original work:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}=-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\log P_{\theta,\psi}\!\left(c_{s}=g_{s}\mid c_{s-1}=g_{s-1}\right).
``` |  | (8) |

The original work complements $`\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}`$ with a
distractor penalty, which penalizes the probability the head assigns to
candidates that the target ranks below the ground truth, in proportion
to the target’s log-probability gap. We replace it with a calibration
term. Let $`q_{s,k}=p_{M}\!\left(v_{s,k}\mid x_{<t+s}\right)`$ be the
target probability of $`v_{s,k}`$, the token of the $`k`$-th candidate
at slot $`s`$, and
$`\bar{q}_{s,k}=q_{s,k}/\sum_{k^{\prime}}q_{s,k^{\prime}}`$ its
renormalization over the $`K`$ candidates. The calibration term is the
cross-entropy from $`\bar{q}`$ to the head’s per-slot distribution:

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cal}}=-\frac{1}{|\mathcal{S}|}\sum_{s\in\mathcal{S}}\sum_{k=1}^{K}\bar{q}_{s,k}\log P_{\theta,\psi}\!\left(c_{s}=k\mid c_{s-1}=g_{s-1}\right).
``` |  | (9) |

The distractor penalty is minimized by any distribution that places all
of its probability on candidates the target ranks at or above the ground
truth. The calibration term instead has a unique minimizer, the target’s
renormalized distribution $`\bar{q}_{s,\cdot}`$ over the candidates of
each slot. This matters under probabilistic draft sampling, which at
$`temp=1`$ draws each draft token from the head’s per-slot distribution.
The drafter body and the head are trained jointly on

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  |
``` math
\mathcal{L}=\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{DFlash}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{CE}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}+\lambda_{\mathrm{cal}}\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cal}},
``` |  | (10) |

where the weights $`\lambda_{\mathrm{CE}}`$ and
$`\lambda_{\mathrm{cal}}`$ are given in Table
[27](#S5.T27 "Table 27 ‣ Hyperparameters. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

##### Data.

We train in two stages, which draw on the same $`6.32`$M prompts from
$`12`$ Nemotron post-training datasets, listed in Table
[26](#S5.T26 "Table 26 ‣ Data. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
and differ in the checkpoint that generates the responses and serves as
the streaming target. In the first stage this is a post-SFT Nemotron 3.5
Super checkpoint. In the second it is the released post-RL checkpoint,
so that the drafter adapts to the hidden states and output distribution
of the model it serves. In the first stage a randomly selected subset of
the assistant turns of each sample is regenerated, and each sample ends
at a regenerated turn. In the second stage all assistant turns are
regenerated. Generation uses reasoning, temperature $`1.0`$ with
top-$`p`$ of $`0.95`$, and is limited to $`8{,}192`$ new tokens. User
turns and tool results are kept verbatim, and no tool is executed. The
loss is applied only to the final assistant turn, which is always a
regenerated one. The first stage trains on windows of $`4{,}096`$ tokens
and the second on windows of $`8{,}192`$ tokens. After filtering, the
first stage retains $`5.67`$M conversations totaling $`22.6`$B tokens,
of which $`14.9`$B fall within its training window, and the second stage
retains $`11.01`$M of $`18.2`$M generated samples totaling $`35.6`$B
tokens, of which $`34.9`$B fall within its training window.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Dataset |  Splits | Prompts |
| [Nemotron-Competitive-Programming-v1](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-Competitive-Programming-v1) |  InfiniByte | $`1{,}174{,}694`$ |
| [Nemotron-SFT-Agentic-v2](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-SFT-Agentic-v2) |  interactive agent, search, tool calling | $`991{,}900`$ |
| [Nemotron-SFT-Instruction-Following-Chat-v2](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-SFT-Instruction-Following-Chat-v2) |  reasoning on | $`929{,}237`$ |
| [Nemotron-Post-Training-Dataset-v2](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-Post-Training-Dataset-v2) |  chat, code, math, STEM, multilingual^(†) | $`800{,}000`$ |
| [Nemotron-RL-Super-Training-Blends](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-RL-Super-Training-Blends) |  RLHF, RLVR, SWE | $`479{,}303`$ |
| [Nemotron-SFT-OpenCode-v1](https://huggingface.co/datasets/nvidia/Nemotron-SFT-OpenCode-v1) |  all six subsets | $`460{,}254`$ |

| Total |  | $`6{,}324{,}762`$ |

first stage retains $`5.67`$M conversations and $`22.6`$B tokens, of
which $`14.9`$B fall within its $`4{,}096`$-token training window. The
second stage retains $`11.01`$M of $`18.2`$M generated samples and

##### Hyperparameters.

The first stage trains from random initialization for $`3`$ epochs of
$`88{,}539`$ steps each, $`265{,}617`$ steps in total, on
$`4{,}096`$-token windows. The second stage is initialized from the
full-precision weights at the end of the first stage and trains for
$`500{,}000`$ steps, about $`2.9`$ epochs of $`172{,}037`$ steps each,
on $`8{,}192`$-token windows, with a freshly initialized optimizer and
schedule. The target is served with four replicas in the first stage and
eight in the second. Table
[27](#S5.T27 "Table 27 ‣ Hyperparameters. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
compares the configuration with that of the original work.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Setting | Original ([Rusanovsky et al., 2026](#bib.bib70)) | Nemotron 3.5 Super |
| Block size (drafted slots) | $`16`$ ($`15`$) | $`8`$ ($`7`$) |
| Candidates per slot $`K`$ | $`8`$ | $`8`$ |
| Head layers, width, heads, FFN | $`2`$, $`1024`$, $`8`$, $`2048`$ | $`2`$, $`1024`$, $`8`$, $`2048`$ |
| Cosine scale $`\sigma`$ | $`8`$ | $`8`$ |
| Body key-value heads | $`8`$ | $`8`$ |
| Body convolution (kernel, group) | none | $`2`$, $`16`$ |
| Body loss | cross-entropy | distillation, Eq. ([6](#S5.E6 "In Objective. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")) |
| Position decay $`\beta`$ | $`7`$ | $`4`$ |
| Head loss | $`0.25\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}+0.25\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{dist}}`$ | $`0.05\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{CE}}+0.45\,\mathcal{L}_{\mathrm{cal}}`$ |
| Optimizer | AdamW | AdamW |
| Learning rate | $`6\times 10^{-4}`$ | $`8\times 10^{-4}`$ |
| Schedule | linear, $`4\%`$ warmup | constant, $`1{,}000`$-step warmup |
| Weight decay, gradient clipping | $`0`$, $`1.0`$ | $`0`$, $`1.0`$ |
| Global batch, sequence length | $`64`$, $`3{,}120`$ | $`64`$, $`4{,}096`$ / $`8{,}192`$ (stage 1 / 2) |
| Blocks sampled per sequence | $`512`$ | $`512`$ |
| Training epochs (stage 1 $`+`$ stage 2) | $`6`$ | $`3`$ $`+`$ $`2.9`$ ($`500`$K steps) |
| Precision | BF16, FP32 master weights | BF16, FP32 master weights |
| Hardware | $`64\times`$ H100 | $`96\times`$ GB200 ($`64`$ train, $`32`$ target) |

Table 27: Training configuration of LiLiCorr for Nemotron 3.5 Super,
compared with the original work.

##### Evaluation

We evaluate on the qualitative split of SPEED-Bench ([Abramovich et al.,
2026b](#bib.bib2)), which comprises $`880`$ prompts, $`80`$ in each of
$`11`$ categories. The drafter is served with the LiLiCorr integration
of vLLM ([Kwon et al., 2023](#bib.bib42))²⁷²⁷ 27
[https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57934](https://github.com/vllm-project/vllm/pull/57934)
with a draft length of $`7`$ and a concurrency of $`64`$. We report the
per-sample acceptance length (AL), i.e., the number of tokens accepted
per verification step, including the token contributed by the target,
averaged over each request and then over requests. $`temp=0`$ is greedy
decoding, in which the drafter commits the walk of Eq.
([5](#S5.E5 "In Introduction. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
$`temp=1`$, with top-$`p`$ of $`0.95`$, uses probabilistic draft
sampling from Eq.
([7](#S5.E7 "In Objective. ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")).
For both settings we report the mean and standard deviation over three
seeds. We use NVFP4 version of Nemotron 3.5 Super model for verification
since it is most useful for inference and hence in combination with the
drafter.

Because the drafter is trained on windows of at most $`8{,}192`$ tokens,
its attention does not extrapolate to longer contexts, and without
adaptation its acceptance degrades sharply with input length. We
therefore serve it with sliding-window attention over the most recent
$`8{,}192`$ positions in every drafter layer, so that query-key
distances never exceed those seen in training. On inputs shorter than
the window this is identical to full attention. Since nearly all
SPEED-Bench qualitative samples fit within the window, the numbers in
Table
[28](#S5.T28 "Table 28 ‣ Evaluation ‣ 5.7.1 Training LiLiCorr for Super3.5 ‣ 5.7 Speculative Decoding with Parallel Drafters ‣ 5 Inference ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
are effectively those of full attention.

Table 28: Average acceptance length (AL) of LiLiCorr on the SPEED-Bench
qualitative split, at draft length $`7`$, served in vLLM at concurrency
$`64`$ with $`8{,}192`$-token sliding-window attention, verified by the
NVFP4 Nemotron 3.5 Super. $`temp=0`$ is greedy decoding, and $`temp=1`$

## 6 Conclusion

We presented Nemotron 3.5 Super and Nemotron 3.5 Lightning, efficient
Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer models designed for
reasoning, long-context understanding, and agentic workloads. Through
large-scale distillation, long-context training, and native Multi-Token
Prediction (MTP), both models combine strong capabilities with efficient
inference. Nemotron 3.5 Super further incorporates Indic continued
pretraining and agentic software-engineering mid-training, while a
comprehensive post-training pipeline spanning supervised fine-tuning,
reinforcement learning, specialized teacher training, and multi-teacher
on-policy distillation transforms it into a multimodal agent. Nemotron
3.5 Lightning follows a lighter text-only post-training pipeline
optimized for efficient deployment.

Our results demonstrate improvements across capability and efficiency.
Indic continued pretraining improves Indian-language benchmarks by up to
9.82 percentage points, while agentic mid-training strengthens
long-horizon software engineering and robustness across agent harnesses.
The final Super model achieves 51.78% on SWE-bench Pro, outperforming
Nemotron 3 Ultra despite using approximately 4.6$`\times`$ fewer active
parameters. Test-time scaling through parallel agent execution and
self-verification further improves Terminal-Bench 2.1 from 62.7% to
71.9%. On the efficiency side, post-training MTP boosting improves
speculative-decoding acceptance length by 10.1% under greedy decoding
and 21.8% under probabilistic draft sampling, while NVFP4 quantization
achieves 99.52% median accuracy recovery relative to BF16. On GB200,
Super achieves 1.9$`\times`$ the serving throughput of Qwen3.8
Flash-Next under the evaluated interactive agentic workload.

Together, these results demonstrate the benefits of jointly optimizing
pretraining, post-training, and inference for capable and efficient
agentic models. We release model checkpoints, datasets, and supporting
recipes and cookbooks to enable further research and development of open
agentic AI systems.

## Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization

### B.1 Runaway loop: the termination failure in truncated rollouts

Reasoning length is not a defect; long reasoning in an agentic workload
is mostly probing such as running code, invoking tools, and reading back
the result. A model’s abstention can fail by two mechanisms: *knowledge*
collapse, which produces hallucination, and *reasoning* collapse, which
produces termination failure, seen as semantic runaway looping behavior.
Qualitatively, the runaway loop is recognizable as *failed negative
proof*: the model cannot certify a negative result from exhaustive
investigation, but confidently reopens a settled conclusion and cycles.
Therefore, a non-terminating agent emits more calls of ordinary length,
with per-call statistics steady, rather than longer calls. Eventually,
the rollout’s last reasoning starts exploding in length, or the
truncation rate increases.

We identify the runaway loop by reading reasoning passages in
postmortem, while tracking the failure of a model to terminate as it
runs to the context wall. Table
[31](#A2.T31 "Table 31 ‣ B.1 Runaway loop: the termination failure in truncated rollouts ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
reports the two immediately measurable quantities: truncation, the
fraction of *rollouts* containing at least one model call hitting an
output limit, and output length per rollout. The combination of these
two quantities is a useful early-warning indicator. For example,
tau3-banking has 20.9% truncation and 35,173 output tokens per rollout
before MOPD, where we detect 19.7% runaway loop failures (153/776
rollouts). After MOPD, truncation decreases to 0.3% with 13,669 output
tokens per rollout, where only one runaway loop failure is captured.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| Benchmark | Modality | Rollouts | ISL (k) | OSL (k) / rollout | Trunc. % |
| Baby Vision | vision | $`388\times 3`$ | 0.5 | 42.3 $`\rightarrow`$ 11.2 (0.27$`\times`$) | 39.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 1.9 |
| CharXiv (RQ) | vision | $`1{,}000\times 3`$ | 3.1 | 11.8 $`\rightarrow`$ 3.3 (0.28$`\times`$) | 9.2 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.6 |
| MMLongBench-Doc | vision | $`1{,}091\times 3`$ | 75.0 | 10.8 $`\rightarrow`$ 3.0 (0.28$`\times`$) | 9.9 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.4 |
| OCR Reasoning | vision | $`1{,}069\times 3`$ | 1.5–1.6 ^(a) | 16.8 $`\rightarrow`$ 5.2 (0.31$`\times`$) | 13.7 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.6 |
| OSWorld | vision/agentic | $`361\times 3`$ | — | 13.6 $`\rightarrow`$ 5.1 (0.38$`\times`$) | 1.2 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.0 |
| Tau3 Banking | text/agentic | $`97\times 8`$ | — | 35.2 $`\rightarrow`$ 13.7 (0.39$`\times`$) | 20.9 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.3 |
| AA-Omniscience | text | $`600\times 10`$ | 0.1 | 10.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 5.6 (0.56$`\times`$) | 1.9 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.3 |
| SciCode | text | $`65\times 8`$ | 3.0–3.1 ^(a) | 7.2 $`\rightarrow`$ 6.5 (0.90$`\times`$) | 0.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.0 |
| AA-LCR | text | $`100\times 16`$ | 108.0 | 4.7 $`\rightarrow`$ 4.7 (1.00$`\times`$) | 0.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.2 |
| Tau2-AA | text/agentic | $`278\times 8`$ | — | 5.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 5.1 (1.03$`\times`$) | 0.0 $`\rightarrow`$ 0.0 |

^(a) evaluation harness update resulted in ISL difference.

Table 31: Output length per rollout and truncation, pre-MOPD versus
MOPD, both in BF16. OSL is the total over a rollout’s *policy-model*
calls, excluding the user simulator. Post-training RL removed the
non-terminating tail, not baseline verbosity.

### B.2 FP4 weight quantization swings the stop-versus-continue margin

Quantized models inherit the runaway loop behavior from the base model,
and quantization can make it worse. We divide and conquer the
degradation by ablating non-attention components in pre-MOPD models:

- •
  Routed/shared experts NVFP4 $`\rightarrow`$ FP8. In tau3-banking,
  runaway dropped from 28.2% $`\rightarrow`$ 17.9%, below the BF16
  reference’s 19.7%; output verbosity decreased by 21.5% at
  2.7$`\sigma`$. This rules out expert weights being innocent.
- •
  Expert *activations* $`\rightarrow`$ BF16. Runaway moves by
  $`\approx 0`$; output verbosity falls on 5 of 5 focused benchmarks
  (mmlongbench-doc, scicode, tau3-banking, tau2-AA, AA-LCR). This
  eliminates the activation path as the carrier of the termination
  failure.
- •
  lm_head returned to BF16. No recovery of termination is captured. This
  eliminates the final output token generation as the source of the
  runaway loop.
- •
  W4A4 expert GEMM output error. The relative L2 norm error is estimated
  to be 7.5–22.9% against BF16, which is above 10% in 39 of 40 MoE
  layers, and 15% on average. This eliminates “the small perturbation
  can be ignored”.
- •
  Mamba output projection $`\rightarrow`$ W4A16. In tau3-banking, output
  verbosity increased by 18.5% at 2.4$`\sigma`$, while the runaway rate
  and knowledge abstention were both unaffected.

By putting all the pieces together, we hypothesize the culprit is the
quantization of the expert weights. Promoting expert weights to FP8
mitigates the runaway loop on pre-MOPD models, while post-MOPD model
quantization successfully recovers the verbosity without runaway loop
regression in FP4 expert weights. The Mamba result carries over to the
post-MOPD checkpoints: against a quantization recipe keeping output
projection at W8A8, W4A16 is more verbose on all 6 benchmark tests.
Please refer to Appendix
[B.4](#A2.SS4 "B.4 Mamba output projection: FP4 weights, and the sensitivity is on generation length ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
Note that a perturbation from FP4 expert weights does not destroy
information, so knowledge abstention is not affected across all
experiments. AA-OmniScience non-hallucination spans only $`0.991\times`$
to $`1.022\times`$ across the five ablations, which corresponds to
$`67.8\%`$ and $`69.9\%`$ against BF16’s $`68.4\%`$.

##### Where in the stack: the decision rides on a weak signal.

Table
[32](#A2.T32 "Table 32 ‣ Where in the stack: the decision rides on a weak signal. ‣ B.2 FP4 weight quantization swings the stop-versus-continue margin ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
depicts a decomposition of each MoE layer’s output into its routed and
shared experts, with $`\alpha=\|\text{routed}\|/\|\text{layer}\|`$ and
$`\beta=\|\text{shared}\|/\|\text{layer}\|`$. This magnitude analysis
reveals a crossover at layer 59: the routed expert’s contribution
roughly halves while the shared one rises, and the shared experts
dominate nearly every layer from 59 to 83. Interestingly, when we split
rollouts by whether BF16 and the FP4 quantized expert weights produce
the runaway loop effect from pre-MOPD models, we see a clear trend of
relatively weaker routed expert contribution in the late band, if a
runaway loop occurs. This effect appears in both BF16 and FP4
quantization. For example, on rollouts where only BF16 experts loop, the
routed expert contribution is $`0.055`$ under BF16 but $`0.093`$ under
the FP4 quantized experts, whereas on rollouts where only the FP4
quantized experts loop, the routed expert contribution is $`0.091`$
under BF16 but $`0.061`$ under FP4. It is worth noting that FP4 weight
quantization error is uniform: FP4 expert weights carry a relative error
of $`0.087`$ against BF16, in contrast to FP8 expert quantization’s
$`0.027`$ across all layers. The weak routed contribution is a signature
of the looping state. Under low-amplitude signals, FP4 quantization
flips are margin-confined: they occur where many continuations are
interchangeable, and the flip redirects output generations without
changing their knowledge. The stop-versus-continue decision itself is
far from an ordinary continuation, but a small shift in the probability
of stopping at ordinary positions attenuates termination across a long
generation. That is also why accuracy stays flat while verbosity moves
on those five benchmarks.

|----|----|----|----|----|----|
| pre-MOPD Runaway? |  | L59–L83 (12) $`\alpha/\beta`$ under |  |  |  |
| BF16 | FP4 | pre-MOPD BF16 | pre-MOPD FP4 | MOPD BF16 | MOPD FP4 |
| No | No | 0.100/0.137 | 0.097/0.146 | 0.100/0.142 | 0.097/0.140 |
| No | Yes | 0.091/0.144 | 0.061/0.162 | 0.092/0.149 | 0.092/0.147 |
| Yes | No | 0.055/0.161 | 0.093/0.139 | 0.081/0.164 | 0.081/0.161 |
| Yes | Yes | 0.059/0.151 | 0.063/0.155 | 0.077/0.160 | 0.078/0.155 |

contributions in 12 MoE layers of L59–L83, split by whether each

versus pre-MOPD FP4 rollout. MOPD rollouts reported 1 runaway loop
failure in BF16, and 3 failures in FP4. All MoE layers before L59 agree
to within $`0.031`$ regardless of pre-MOPD versus MOPD, and BF16 versus
FP4 quantization.

On the other hand, MOPD fortifies the latent support for stopping enough
to withstand FP4 quantization noise. Under MOPD, which loops on only 1
of 776 of these rollouts, the same prompts recover to $`0.081`$ and
$`0.077`$ while $`\beta`$ stays at the elevated $`0.16`$, and FP4 expert
weight quantization leaves them at $`0.081`$ and $`0.078`$, where
runaway loop failures are reported in 3 cases only. Table
[33](#A2.T33 "Table 33 ‣ Where in the stack: the decision rides on a weak signal. ‣ B.2 FP4 weight quantization swings the stop-versus-continue margin ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
shows that reading the unembedding’s decision directly confirms this: at
the position where \</think\> is actually emitted, $`P(\text{stop})`$ is
$`0.99`$ against $`10^{-9}`$ or less elsewhere, and FP4 expert weights
never cost a termination. FP4 experts affect the *direction* of the
perturbation. It moves away from stopping on 17 of 23 pre-MOPD rollouts,
but toward stopping on 20 of 24 under MOPD. On the runaway rollouts
themselves, pre-MOPD’s peak stop probability has median $`0.043`$ and
the reasoning block never closes, while MOPD reaches median $`0.729`$
and closes the block in 6 of 10. GEMM error could not capture this
difference because it covers the magnitude only, not the distribution
and bias.

[39](#A2.T39 "Table 39 ‣ B.5 The unembedding: FP8 weights, and the sensitivity is on termination ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").
The first three rows are measured on the rollouts where \</think\> was
emitted. The last two rows are measured on the rollouts causing a

$`p=2^{1-n}\sum_{i=k}^{n}\binom{n}{i}`$, $`k`$ out of $`n`$ rollouts.

### B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections

From the viewpoint of dense attention, long-context retrieval has three
different perspectives on the decision:

- •
  Distinction: the target is identifiable by its content. The model must
  match on what a candidate says, wherever it sits.
- •
  Disambiguation: the candidates are near-identical, so content cannot
  separate them. The model must count or index occurrences by ordinal
  position.
- •
  Aggregation: there is no single target; evidence must be combined
  across many candidates.

Table
[19](#S4.T19 "Table 19 ‣ 4.6 Attention and Projection Precision ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
maps the examples of long-context benchmarks onto these three and
reports the result. As noted, quantization costs 2.65 to 3.6 pp of
disambiguation and nothing measurable elsewhere. The same effect is also
observed on Nemotron 3.5 Lightning. This section focuses on Super 3.5 VL
to understand the mechanism.

##### Attention score dilution occurs in long-context retrieval.

Attention scores are resolution-starved before any projection error is
added. A softmax output lies in $`0\leq P\leq 1`$, where a
floating-point format mostly carries the magnitude in its exponent. As
attention delivers $`O=PV`$, a logit perturbation is a *bit shift* on
the $`v`$ vector. Table
[34](#A2.T34 "Table 34 ‣ Attention score dilution occurs in long-context retrieval. ‣ B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
describes measuring per head across 12 contexts spanning 10,532 to
63,641 prefix tokens, where 4,730 to 7,981 keys are co-located in the
attention window. These keys compete for only 35 to 80 distinct e4m3
score levels, putting 60 to 120 keys on each level.

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Quantity | Measured | Reading |
| Keys in representable window | 4,730–7,981 | population competing |
| Distinct e4m3 $`P`$ levels | 35–80 | levels available |
| Keys sharing a level | 60–120 | the dilution |
| Ties at the top e4m3 level | 1–3 | the peak stays separated |
| Attn. score mass preserved | 0.994–1.001 | mass intact, resolution lost |
| Spearman $`\rho`$(context len., keys/level) | $`+0.73`$–$`+0.94`$ | more keys per level when longer |

Table 34: tau3-banking’s score-ladder dilution under an FP8 KV cache,
per head, across 12 contexts of 10,532–63,641 prefix tokens. The softmax
output $`P`$ is cast to e4m3 for the $`PV`$ GEMM; the logits entering
the softmax are not.

Suppose a distinction probe of GraphWalks, which reaches 361k tokens of
observed context. From logit’s exponential property, most per-position
candidates are flat in attention scores. So is document-scale
aggregation. While attention looks for peak score, which is cleanly
separated from other positions, a long-context workload requiring
distinction and aggregation works well with FP8 or NVFP4 KV cache
precision. Unfortunately, if candidates are near-identical by
construction, so nothing but position separates them, the precision
becomes its limitation as demonstrated by MRCR-128k.

##### MRCR-128k stratification: damage is concentrated on specific context/candidate counts.

Even though the total attention mass is preserved, per-level key
concentration destroys the resolution in quantization. As shown in Table
[19](#S4.T19 "Table 19 ‣ 4.6 Attention and Projection Precision ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
the BF16-over-FP8 on $`q/k/v`$ is worth $`0.83`$ pp, with a standard
error of $`0.71`$ pp for MRCR-128k. This might be interpreted as an
insignificant improvement as MXFP8 projection gains $`+0.99`$ pp against
the per-tensor FP8, which places it $`0.16`$ pp on the far side of BF16.
Neither contrast clears $`2\sigma`$(=$`1.4`$ pp) under the standard
variance. Likewise, the last row of Table
[34](#A2.T34 "Table 34 ‣ Attention score dilution occurs in long-context retrieval. ‣ B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
might suggest damage grows smoothly with context length. It does not
grow linearly. Table
[35](#A2.T35 "Table 35 ‣ MRCR-128k stratification: damage is concentrated on specific context/candidate counts. ‣ B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
stratifies 1,470 MRCR-128k tasks at three repetitions to separate the
retrieval from decoding noise. The ablations show the damage is present
at every length, but exacerbated at 32k+: $`q/k/v`$ FP8 retains
$`95.3\%`$ and $`95.5\%`$ below 32k, against $`91.4\%`$ and $`92.1\%`$
above 32k, a $`3.6`$ pp break. On the contrary, $`q/k/v`$ MXFP8
projection recovers $`95.3\%`$ and $`94.3\%`$ above 32k, similar to
$`q/k/v`$ BF16. Substituting MXFP8 for per-tensor FP8 on $`q/k/v`$
changes only the scale granularity with one E8M0 exponent per 32
elements instead of one float per tensor, which is enough to close the
gap. Crowding a level only costs accuracy when the co-resident keys are
confusable, and unrelated context does not threaten a retrieval whose
target has a wide margin.

|         |       |          |            |             |           |
|---------|-------|----------|------------|-------------|-----------|
|         |       | accuracy | recovery % |             |           |
| context | $`n`$ | BF16     | q/k/v BF16 | q/k/v MXFP8 | q/k/v FP8 |
| 0–8k    | 290   | 0.768    | 97.9       | 97.5        | 95.3      |
| 8–32k   | 578   | 0.552    | 94.6       | 95.1        | 95.5      |
| 32–64k  | 295   | 0.559    | 95.0       | 95.3        | 91.4      |
| 64–131k | 307   | 0.596    | 93.8       | 94.3        | 92.1      |

Table 35: MRCR-128k accuracy recovery against the BF16 reference, by
served context, over 1,470 tasks at three repetitions. Context bins use
n_tokens, which undercounts the served prompt by about $`3\%`$. The
$`o`$ projection is FP8 in every recovery column.

On the other hand, Table
[36](#A2.T36 "Table 36 ‣ MRCR-128k stratification: damage is concentrated on specific context/candidate counts. ‣ B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
describes the BF16 collapse from 0.800 to 0.378 as the candidate count
rises from two to eight, and that ordering holds inside every context
band. Context also affects the damage, but its impact is not
monotonically increasing: the baseline accuracy steps down by $`13.4`$
to $`29.5`$ pp between 0–8k and 8–32k and then saturates, so above 32k
the candidate count is the dominant factor. This is the signature of an
exhausted indexing budget rather than a strained context window.

|  |  |  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | BF16 accuracy by served context |  |  |  | pooled |  |
| candidates | 0–8k | 8–32k | 32–64k | 64–131k | BF16 | recovery % |
| 2 | 0.883 | 0.749 | 0.793 | 0.823 | 0.800 | 94.9 |
| 4 | 0.806 | 0.585 | 0.522 | 0.694 | 0.637 | 91.7 |
| 8 | 0.616 | 0.320 | 0.353 | 0.290 | 0.378 | 96.0 |

Table 36: MRCR-128k BF16 accuracy by number of near-identical candidates
$`\times`$ served context, over 1,470 tasks at three repetitions. The
two axes are independent by construction: each context band carries a
$`33\%`$ mix of the three candidate counts, and the candidate strata are
length-matched at $`36.4`$–$`37.8`$k mean prompt tokens. Recovery is
$`q/k/v/o`$ FP8 against the BF16 reference.

In short, the damage is worst in the middle, smaller at two candidates
where the margins are wide enough to absorb the error, and smaller again
at eight where the reference has already collapsed and little remains to
lose. Similar to the unembedding in Appendix
[B.5](#A2.SS5 "B.5 The unembedding: FP8 weights, and the sensitivity is on termination ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
quantization moves outcomes where the decision was already close.
However, attention score is stronger evidence for a resolution
mechanism: one that merely made everything harder would not spare the
hardest stratum. From this observation, reporting a single number hides
the damage in long-context benchmarks. It is important to stratify test
cases along with *capability partition* to assess quantization damage
properly.

##### The attention score has FP8 resolution at per-block scale granularity.

On Nemotron 3.5 Lightning model, when we move the KV cache from FP8 to
BF16 while keeping weights and projections fixed, MRCR-128k’s accuracy
recovers $`+1.43`$ pp (=$`2.4\sigma`$); while moving the projections
from FP8 to BF16, it recovers $`+2.97`$ pp (=$`2.8\sigma`$). On Nemotron
3.5 Super VL model the impact of projection precision appears from our
ablations. While KV cache is the damage carrier, high-precision
$`q/k/v`$ projection does not prevent dilution; it buys back part of the
margin destroyed by the cast. Table
[37](#A2.T37 "Table 37 ‣ The attention score has FP8 resolution at per-block scale granularity. ‣ B.3 The damage of long-context retrieval on attention projections ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
varies attention projection precision on AA-LCR, an aggregation
benchmark serving 108k tokens of context on average. As noted,
aggregation benchmarks can identify important candidates in FP8, where
even per-tensor FP8 precision can be acceptable over 100 tasks at
sixteen repetitions. It also states accuracy drop of $`2.50`$ pp from
all-FP8 to NVFP4 projections, with $`\mathrm{SE}=1.11`$. Across the
$`24`$ $`q/k/v`$ weight tensors, the spread of per-16-element block
maxima is $`2.51`$ binades at the median and $`8.78`$ at worst, and the
activations entering those projections span $`1.67`$, which is against
the $`17.81`$ binades an E4M3 block scale encodes before it underflows.
No block in the model loses its scale, leaving $`9.03`$ binades unused
on the worst tensor. Per-block granularity is not reclaiming dynamic
range, but recovers precision headroom that a per-tensor scale had
exhausted. For example, under a fused $`q/k/v`$ FP8 projection with a
single shared scale, the key projection holds it at six of eight
attention layers and the value projection at the other two. Fusion adds
roughly 32 % relative weight error to $`q`$ and $`v`$ against 4 % for
$`k`$, which is pure rounding noise.

|                                     |        |                             |
|-------------------------------------|--------|-----------------------------|
| Attention $`\{q,k,v,o\}`$ precision | Score  | $`\Delta`$ vs BF16 baseline |
| All four at NVFP4                   | 0.5713 | $`-2.25`$ pp                |
| All four BF16                       | 0.5900 | $`-0.38`$ pp                |
| All four FP8                        | 0.5962 | $`+0.25`$ pp                |
| $`o`$ FP8, $`q/k/v`$ BF16           | 0.6031 | $`+0.94`$ pp                |
| $`q/k/v`$ FP8, $`o`$ BF16           | 0.6050 | $`+1.12`$ pp                |

Table 37: Ablation of attention projection precision on AA-LCR. This
benchmark does not exercise disambiguation.

This study recommends FP8 precision on $`q/k/v`$ projection *at
per-block scale granularity*. If deployment does not perform
disambiguation-style retrieval, however, no other workload would reveal
a significant difference. After the softmax has chosen the target $`v`$
vectors, the head outputs are passed to the output projection. Note that
the spread of output projection weights is 4.21 binades in median and
8.15 in the worst case across eight attention layers, where output
projection itself is not exposed to FP8 underflow. On the activation
side, the calibrated per-tensor input scale is equally well-behaved: it
sits at 49–76 % of the largest head output norm at every attention
layer, holding that fraction across a $`68\times`$ range of absolute
magnitude. Counterintuitively, the concatenation of head outputs under a
single per-tensor input scale does not produce a benchmark-level penalty
at FP8 precision. We claim that the output projection is structurally
exposed and measurably tolerant in per-tensor FP8. In the meantime, it
is worth noting that the mixed-precision checkpoints of
§[4.3](#S4.SS3 "4.3 Sensitivity-Driven Mixed Precision ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
produced by a gradient-based per-layer search, follow these findings:
the *AutoQuant* search leaves $`q/k/v`$ in BF16 on three of the eight
attention layers under both the 4.85 and 4.90 bit-per-parameter budgets,
on the same three layers in each, while $`o`$ stays FP8 on every
attention layer under 4.85 and reaches BF16 on three layers only once
the budget loosens to 4.90.

### B.4 Mamba output projection: FP4 weights, and the sensitivity is on generation length

The Mamba output projection differs from the expert fc2 in the input
*shape* of the nonlinearity. Expert fc2 is fed by a squared-ReLU
activation, where 90–99 % of its input elements are exactly zero before
quantization, and only 1.05–3.32 values survive per FP4 block. Mamba SSM
cache passes a normalized SiLU gate to the output projection, which
provides near-zero values but never exactly zero. Consequently, the
input of Mamba output projection carries near 16 survivors per FP4
block, and $`27.6\%`$ of elements fall below the
$`\text{amax}_{\text{block}}/24`$ rounding limit under FP4 activation
quantization. This tensor analysis stops us from taking the Mamba output
projection to W4A4. Applying rotation or higher activation precision can
mitigate the flush.

We evaluated the impact of FP4 weight quantization with W4A16 against
FP8 W8A8. Due to high precision on activation, this contrast reveals the
weight quantization effect directly. Table
[38](#A2.T38 "Table 38 ‣ B.4 Mamba output projection: FP4 weights, and the sensitivity is on generation length ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
shows the output verbosity inflation caused by FP4 weight quantization.
All six benchmarks move in the same direction, with OSWorld and SciCode
carrying the clearest inflation at $`1.27\times`$ and $`1.16\times`$.
Note that each task accuracy is unchanged across two recipes. To address
this issue, we preferred parameter alignment as described in
§[4.4](#S4.SS4 "4.4 Quantization-Aware Distillation (QAD) ‣ 4 Quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI").

|                |            |               |                |
|----------------|------------|---------------|----------------|
| Benchmark      | FP8 (W8A8) | NVFP4 (W4A16) | ratio          |
| OSWorld        | 8.3k       | 10.6k         | $`1.27\times`$ |
| SciCode        | 12.0k      | 13.9k         | $`1.16\times`$ |
| Tau3 Banking   | 14.3k      | 15.5k         | $`1.08\times`$ |
| AA-Omniscience | 6.4k       | 6.7k          | $`1.06\times`$ |
| AA-LCR         | 5.7k       | 5.9k          | $`1.02\times`$ |
| Tau2-AA        | 5.2k       | 5.2k          | $`1.01\times`$ |

Table 38: output generation length per rollout for a quantization recipe
pair differing in Mamba output projection only: W8A8 versus W4A16.
Counts are policy-model output, excluding the user simulator on the
agentic benchmarks.

### B.5 The unembedding: FP8 weights, and the sensitivity is on termination

The unembedding should stay at FP8 or above. As described in Table
[39](#A2.T39 "Table 39 ‣ B.5 The unembedding: FP8 weights, and the sensitivity is on termination ‣ Appendix B Ablation study of post-training quantization ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI"),
NVFP4 lm_head also holds high top-1 agreement with BF16. FP4’s problem
is a failure of the stop-versus-continue decision, which results in
output verbosity inflation.

|  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|
|  | FP8 (W8A16) | NVFP4 (W4A16) | Ratio |
| Weight error | 0.0265 | 0.0952 | 3.6$`\times`$ |
| Top-1 agreement with BF16 | 98.6 % | 95.8 % | — |
| KL mean / p99 (bits) | 0.0013 / 0.0080 | 0.0121 / 0.0797 | 9.3$`\times`$ / 10$`\times`$ |
| Stop/continue flip rate | 0.781 % | 2.450 % | 3.14$`\times`$ |

Table 39: Unembedding sensitivity, per-tensor FP8 versus NVFP4, measured
on an identical hidden-state input with fake quantization.

We evaluate the decision as a single logit comparison,
$`\text{margin}=h\cdot(v_{\text{stop}}-v_{\text{cont}})`$. As \</think\>
is one token, everything shared by the two unembedding rows cancels out.
After quantization, the \</think\> row provides a cosine similarity of
0.9961 to the original. The vector barely moves, however 2.45 % of
decisions flip due to the other vectors providing $`\cos\approx 0.22`$.
This is because the decision lives in the *difference* of two
nearly-orthogonal rows, where a small rotation of each becomes a large
perturbation of their difference. “The weights look fine” and “the
decision flipped” are both true. We rejected a hypothesis of
quantization damage from frequency-skewed rare-token distribution in
four ways:

1.  1.
    Row-amax spread is 2.84 binades (2.12 binades at p99.9) against
    FP8’s roughly 17.8 usable, so there is nothing to reclaim on
    per-block scale.
2.  2.
    Tested error ranging from group size of 1 through 64 elements, and
    the pooled error is identical to four decimal places per each
    format, i.e., 0.0265 in FP8, and 0.0952 in FP4.
3.  3.
    Per-row median spread by vocabulary-frequency quartile is 5.222 /
    5.246 / 5.252 / 5.274 binades. In other words, a 0.05-binade drift
    across 131,072 tokens. There are zero dead rows in every
    configuration, and the lowest-norm decile of relative quantization
    error is no worse than the highest: 0.0941 versus 0.0952. The error
    is uniformly distributed across the vocabulary.
4.  4.
    NVFP4 crosses its flush line on 66.9 % of blocks, flushing 6.8 % of
    elements, but this flush carries no vocabulary structure at any
    density level. Whatever it costs, it costs everywhere, so FP4
    quantization cannot produce a loss concentrated in rare tokens.

The remaining hypothesis is per-element *grid*: E4M3 carries three
mantissa bits providing a resolution of 12.5 % step size
($`1\rightarrow 1.125`$), whereas E2M1 carries one, providing a
resolution of 50 % step size ($`1\rightarrow 1.5`$). To support this
hypothesis, we emulated two-pass FP4 weights, where a second channel
corrects the residual left below the $`\text{amax}/24`$ flush line. This
correction recovers the gap entirely: with a linear E2M1 residual the KL
falls to $`0.00117`$ against per-tensor FP8’s $`0.00134`$, and top-1
agreement with BF16 reaches $`0.9951`$. Reducing the quantization error
in lm_head is still open to exploration, but this suggests that the
unembedding’s numerical sensitivity is confined in FP8 precision.

### B.6 A precision recommendation with limitations

The same quantization recipe, which aggravated the termination failure
before RL post-training, reduced it afterwards. However, it does not
modify model’s inherent structural effect. Across three checkpoints
spanning pre-RL and post-RL training, the stop-versus-continue geometry
is invariant to within 1 % (cosine 0.997, margin 0.996, noise-to-margin
1.001), while termination behavior changed significantly. Together with
the contribution profile, which is invariant to within 0.007 in routed
experts, this gives a clean statement: RL post-training did not move the
lm_head or the expert contribution structure; it changed where the
hidden state points. A precision recommendation can therefore be stated
in structural terms, but its *margin* protection must be
checkpoint-dependent. Here are limitations of this study:

1.  1.
    The weight-versus-activation attribution for the termination failure
    is an inference from a bound: we did not experiment with W16A4.
2.  2.
    Expert GEMM output error and benchmark verbosity are two separate
    measurements, consistent in sign and magnitude. Nothing traces a
    specific displacement to a specific extra token; the relationship is
    coherent, not demonstrated causal.
3.  3.
    The attention score-path dilution is measured on clean weights as
    *structure*, not as a perturbation response.
4.  4.
    Every configuration in this ablation study, including the BF16
    reference, serves an FP8 KV cache; the ladder dilution is therefore
    present in the baseline too, and the projection deltas are measured
    on top of an already-diluted score.
5.  5.
    The MRCR-128k’s disambiguation loss is measured against an
    unquantized reference with an FP8 KV cache, but quantization uses a
    full configuration: FP8 KV cache, FP4 routed experts and FP4 Mamba
    output projection. The KV cache is identified as the damage carrier
    on Nemotron 3.5 Lightning study, but Nemotron 3.5 Super VL ablation
    does not address BF16 KV precision to contrast its damage.
6.  6.
    MRCR-128k is the only disambiguation instrument in the suite, so
    that limb of the result rests on one benchmark. It carries three
    repetitions per task, which is enough to measure the decoding-noise
    floor but not to support per-stratum claims beyond the reported
    candidate-count split.
7.  7.
    The unembedding measurements use a proxy input,
    $`\text{norm}_{f}(\text{hidden}+\text{residual})`$ at the last
    layer, i.e., the true input minus that layer’s own MoE contribution.
    Both tests receive the identical input, so the *comparison* isolates
    unembedding quantization exactly; the absolute rates are not the
    model’s true output on those tokens.
8.  8.
    The contribution analysis uses two fixed token windows, one per
    training stage (pre-MOPD versus MOPD). Within a stage the
    BF16-versus-FP4 contrast holds the fixed window to isolate the
    weights, which establishes the invariant contribution profile with
    or without quantization. The cross-stage comparison changes both the
    weights and the window, so it loosely highlights a structural change
    but does not pinpoint the exact root cause.

## Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details

### C.1 Configuration

Table
[40](#A3.T40 "Table 40 ‣ C.1 Configuration ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
lists the settings used for all SFT and RL data. The benchmark side
(question store, n-gram index, and embedding index) is built once and
shared by all runs. The training data are processed in shards on CPU
nodes, with embeddings and judge decisions served by remote endpoints.

|  |  |
|----|----|
| Component | Setting |
| Scored text | user turn (SFT) or prompt (RL), dataset-level boilerplate removed |
| Verbatim overlap | word 8- and 13-grams; shared n-grams $`/`$ n-grams of the benchmark question |
| Embedding model | Qwen3-Embedding-0.6B |
| Chunking | 512-byte windows with 256-byte stride (sample and question) |
| Semantic overlap | maximum cosine similarity over chunk pairs |
| Candidate selection | per question: top 20 by combined score $`+`$ top 2 per signal |
| Judge gate | semantic overlap $`\geq 0.75`$ or verbatim overlap $`\geq 0.5`$ |
| Judge model | Claude Opus 4.6, temperature 0 |
| Judge input | each text capped at 8,000 characters (head and tail kept) |
| Removal rule | contaminating label (Table [41](#A3.T41 "Table 41 ‣ C.3 Judge Prompt ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")) with severity $`\geq 3`$ |

Table 40: Configuration of the decontamination runs.

### C.2 Benchmarks

The data was checked against the following benchmarks: MATH-500,
IFBench, KernelBench, MMLU-Pro, Terminal-Bench 2.0, Terminal-Bench 2.1,
NV Chat Benchmark, Humanity’s Last Exam, GPQA Diamond, CBRN, BBQ,
TIR-Bench, SciCode, AA-Omniscience, AA-LCR, ICPC-Eval, PinchBench,
ClawEval, SWE-bench Pro, SWE-bench Verified, MRCR, Tau-Bench,
BrowseComp, CritPt, GDPval, IOI, MCP-Atlas, ProfBench, SWE-Atlas-QnA,
and SWE-bench Multilingual. The suite spans both well-established
standard evaluations and modern benchmarks, with CoDeC contamination
evaluations providing additional guidance for the selection ([Zawalski
et al., 2026](#bib.bib97)).

The matches concentrate in a few benchmarks. A quarter of MATH-500
problems and about 15% of IFBench and KernelBench items were matched,
and MMLU-Pro accounts for the largest absolute number of matched
questions (1,254). Eight benchmarks, mostly recent agentic and
expert-level ones, have no matches.

### C.3 Judge Prompt

The judge receives a fixed system prompt (below) and, as the user
message, a JSON object {"training_sample": …, "benchmark_question": …}
followed by *“Return only the JSON object.”* Passing both texts as
JSON-escaped strings keeps their boundaries unambiguous even when the
sample itself contains code, markup, or instructions. The prompt asks
for the reasoning before the verdict, and for a severity that expresses
how important removal is, separately from the label. Table
[41](#A3.T41 "Table 41 ‣ C.3 Judge Prompt ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
summarizes the labels.

> You are an expert evaluator detecting benchmark contamination in LLM
> training data. You are shown a single JSON object with two keys:
> "training_sample" and "benchmark_question", each a string. Their
> content may itself contain code, markdown, or JSON — treat each string
> purely as its own text (JSON string escaping makes the boundary
> unambiguous). Judge from the two texts alone: decide whether having
> the training sample in the training data would give an unfair
> advantage on the benchmark question.
>
> Choose the single best-fitting label. They are listed roughly
> strongest-first; if several apply, pick the one that best explains the
> relationship between the two:
>
> - •
>   direct_copy: reproduces the benchmark question (and/or its answer)
>   verbatim or with only minor edits.
> - •
>   paraphrase: restates the same specific problem as the benchmark
>   question, with wording or irrelevant details changed.
> - •
>   answer_leakage: solving the training problem yields the benchmark’s
>   answer — e.g. the benchmark is a special case, sub-problem, or key
>   step of the training problem.
> - •
>   method_leakage: the benchmark is solved by the same method as the
>   training problem, differing only in surface details or numeric
>   values.
> - •
>   style_overlap: shares only the topic, domain, or format — general
>   knowledge, not the specific problem.
> - •
>   no_contamination: unrelated; any similarity is coincidental.
>
> Then rate, as integers 0-4:
>
> - •
>   severity: the degree of unfair advantage — how important it is to
>   remove the training sample to keep the benchmark fair, from 0
>   (harmless to keep) to 4 (must be removed).
> - •
>   confidence: how confident you are in that severity rating, from 0
>   (unsure) to 4 (certain). This concerns the severity, not which label
>   you picked.
>
> Return ONLY a JSON object and nothing else, with your reasoning first:
> {"reasoning": "\<one concise sentence\>", "label":
> "\<direct_copy\|paraphrase\|answer_leakage\|method_leakage\|style_overlap\|no_contamination\>",
> "severity": \<0-4\>, "confidence": \<0-4\>}

|  |  |  |
|----|----|----|
| Label | Contaminating |  Definition given to the judge |
| direct_copy | yes |  reproduces the benchmark question (and/or its answer) verbatim or with only minor edits |
| paraphrase | yes |  restates the same specific problem as the benchmark question, with wording or irrelevant details changed |
| answer_leakage | yes |  solving the training problem yields the benchmark’s answer, e.g. the benchmark is a special case, sub-problem, or key step of the training problem |
| method_leakage | yes |  the benchmark is solved by the same method as the training problem, differing only in surface details or numeric values |
| style_overlap | no |  shares only the topic, domain, or format: general knowledge, not the specific problem |
| no_contamination | no |  unrelated; any similarity is coincidental |

Table 41: Judge labels. A sample is removed if any of its pairs receives
a contaminating label with severity $`\geq 3`$.

### C.4 Synthetic Evaluation

Ground-truth contamination labels do not exist for real data, so we
evaluate the pipeline on synthetic data. We take all questions of a
target benchmark and transform each with 13 augmentations, at four
strengths (0.25, 0.5, 0.75, 0.9) that scale the intensity of the change.
The augmentations are:

- •
  *surface*: verbatim copy, character noise, case flipping, whitespace
  insertion
- •
  *structural*: cropping to the beginning, middle, or end, word dropout,
  local word reordering, burial among other questions
- •
  *semantic*, generated by another LLM: paraphrase (from light rewording
  to a full restyle), translation (into increasingly distant languages),
  and abstraction (progressive generalization of the problem).

Every transformed question is a positive by construction. As negatives
we add unmodified questions from a different benchmark in the same
domain, chosen to be hard to tell apart from the target: SWE-bench Pro
for Terminal-Bench 2.1 (4,361 positives, 731 negatives) and MMLU-Pro for
GPQA Diamond (9,702 positives, 12,032 negatives). The LLM augmentations
are generated with a model different from the judge, so that the judge
cannot recognize its own rewrites.

Table
[42](#A3.T42 "Table 42 ‣ C.4 Synthetic Evaluation ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
reports each signal applied alone, at thresholds that flag no negatives.
The judge recovers most positives with a single false positive in 12,763
negatives. Verbatim overlap recovers about a quarter (essentially the
copies and mild surface edits), and semantic overlap about half. Recall
is lower on GPQA, whose short multiple-choice questions carry less
distinctive text than Terminal-Bench’s long task descriptions.

|  |  |  |  |  |
|----|----|----|----|----|
|  | Terminal-Bench 2.1 |  | GPQA Diamond |  |
| Signal (alone) | Recall (%) | False pos. | Recall (%) | False pos. |
| Verbatim overlap $`\geq 0.5`$ | 26.3 | 0 / 731 | 25.7 | 0 / 12,032 |
| Semantic overlap $`\geq 0.85`$ | 61.2 | 0 / 731 | 47.4 | 0 / 12,032 |
| LLM judge (severity $`\geq 3`$) | 87.5 | 0 / 731 | 79.1 | 1 / 12,032 |

Table 42: Synthetic evaluation: recall on transformed benchmark
questions and false positives on same-domain negatives, for each signal
alone.

Table
[43](#A3.T43 "Table 43 ‣ C.4 Synthetic Evaluation ‣ Appendix C Benchmark Decontamination Details ‣ Nemotron 3.5 Super and Lightning: Open, Efficient Mixture-of-Experts Hybrid Mamba-Transformer Models for Agentic AI")
breaks recall down by augmentation, averaged over strengths. Copies,
case changes, reordering, and burial in longer text are recovered almost
completely, as are paraphrases. Translations are recovered almost
entirely through the judge. The misses concentrate in aggressive crops,
heavy word dropout, and strong abstraction at the highest strengths,
where the transformed text often no longer identifies the source
question. Because every transformed question is counted as a positive
regardless of how far it drifted, these recall values are conservative.

|                              |                        |                  |
|------------------------------|------------------------|------------------|
| Augmentation                 | Terminal-Bench 2.1 (%) | GPQA Diamond (%) |
| Verbatim copy                | 100.0                  | 100.0            |
| Case flipping                | 100.0                  | 100.0            |
| Burial among other questions | 100.0                  | 100.0            |
| Local word reordering        | 99.7                   | 99.4             |
| LLM paraphrase               | 97.2                   | 93.2             |
| LLM translation              | 94.7                   | 79.4             |
| Whitespace noise             | 92.7                   | 93.9             |
| Crop to beginning            | 90.2                   | 68.8             |
| Character noise              | 88.5                   | 84.8             |
| Crop to middle               | 82.6                   | 63.1             |
| Crop to end                  | 80.9                   | 71.1             |
| LLM abstraction              | 73.6                   | 65.0             |
| Word dropout                 | 67.1                   | 55.3             |

Table 43: Synthetic evaluation: recall by augmentation, averaged over
strengths, for the union of the three signals (a positive counts as
recovered only if it is matched to its own source question).

### C.5 Notable Cases

The removed samples illustrate why contamination cannot be reduced to
string matching. We describe a few patterns found in the judge’s
decisions without reproducing the benchmark items.

- •
  *Translated and reformulated problems.* Several MATH-500 problems
  appear in the training data translated into Chinese, some also
  reformulated to ask for a quantity derived from the original answer
  (e.g. the sum of the numerator and denominator of a fraction). Such
  samples share almost no n-grams with the benchmark, yet still teach
  the answer.
- •
  *Cross-lingual variants of chat prompts.* Some benchmark prompts
  reappear in RLHF prompt collections in other languages (Chinese,
  Japanese, Spanish, Polish, Russian, German, French), sometimes with
  changed numbers, as in a widely shared short-distance travel puzzle
  posed with 50 instead of 500 meters.
- •
  *Benchmark tasks inside agentic data.* Terminal-Bench task
  descriptions appear nearly verbatim inside agentic RL prompts,
  prefixed with environment-setup instructions and with different file
  paths.
- •
  *Expert-level questions.* Questions from Humanity’s Last Exam appear
  in math and tool-integrated reasoning data as copies, paraphrases, or
  problems whose solution contains the benchmark answer as an
  intermediate step.

All those cases were prevented from entering the training mix.

### C.6 Limitations

- •
  *Recall is bounded by candidate selection.* The judge only rules on
  pairs selected by the cheap signals. A contaminated sample that is not
  among a question’s top candidates, or that falls below the similarity
  gate, is always kept.
- •
  *Only the problem statement is scored.* Contamination that stem from
  using the same set of answers or other artifacts is not detected.
- •
  *The evaluation is a proxy.* The augmentations approximate, but do not
  exhaust, the ways contamination arises in practice.
````
